cross The Fingerprinting Paradox: Why Sensitivity Undermines Black-Box Model Integrity Verification

Authors: Shahinul Hoque, Farhin Farhad Riya, Jinyuan Sun, Fnu Suya

Abstract: Black-box integrity verification aims to detect unauthorized modifications to outsourced models using only prediction APIs. Existing frameworks seek to improve verification efficiency through sensitive fingerprints: queries whose predictions are more likely to change under tampering than those of genuine inputs. We show that this sensitivity can also help dishonest providers recognize verification queries and selectively conceal tampering. We prove that excess sensitivity relative to genuine inputs lower-bounds statistical distinguishability. We evaluate five integrity verification frameworks across three model architectures, three datasets, and three adversarial knowledge settings. When the deployed framework is known, our fingerprint detectors achieve AUCs at least 0.989, and selective filtering makes all five frameworks require significantly larger estimated query budgets than genuine inputs, reversing their intended efficiency advantage. A query-efficiency advantage remains marginally in a small subset of evaluated settings with poor detection transfer. We further design verification methods under stronger concealment constraints but find that reduced detectability comes with losses in sensitivity or fingerprint-generation success. Our results expose a sensitivity-detectability tension and show that sensitivity alone does not ensure efficient black-box verification against adaptive malicious providers.

cross Denoising Diffusion with Progressive Posterior Sampling

Authors: Zeyu Luo, Shuai Huang

Abstract: Diffusion models provide effective generative priors for solving noisy linear inverse problems. However, existing posterior sampling methods may require singular value decomposition (SVD) of the measurement operator or prior knowledge of the measurement noise variance, while guidance based on local gradients provides only a limited view of posterior geometry. We propose progressive posterior sampling (PPS) with manifold projection, which automatically estimates the measurement noise variance and avoids SVD. During reverse diffusion, PPS constructs a Gaussian approximation to the posterior distribution in closed form and guides a sample drawn from the local prior toward the approximate posterior mean. We then project the guided sample onto a spherical level set that approximates the local noisy image manifold, promoting consistency with the local geometry of the diffusion prior. Experiments on FFHQ and ImageNet across four restoration tasks and three measurement noise levels demonstrate competitive reconstruction accuracy and perceptual quality by PPS.

cross Beyond the Benchmark: Localizing Released Autonomous Driving Models to Real Vehicles

Authors: Morui Zhu, Deyuan Qu, Qi Chen, Qing Yang

Abstract: Released autonomous-driving models achieve strong benchmark performance, yet deploying them on a new real vehicle remains challenging. cross Compressible Representations: Functional Spines in Deep Neural Networks

Authors: Niranjan Rajesh, Meenakshi Khosla

Abstract: Deep networks embed inputs in high-dimensional representations, but how much of this capacity actually drives behavior? We introduce a framework for identifying, at each layer, the subspace of the representation that is functionally sensitive to the task. Using a matrix analogous to the Fisher information but defined over the hidden representation, we rank directions by their influence on downstream computation and project onto the most task-sensitive subspace. Across pretrained vision networks, representations are remarkably compressible: a small fraction of the task-sensitive subspace preserves most performance, often requiring far fewer dimensions than geometric alternatives. We find that this compressed representation is also more robust to adversarial attacks generated on the full network. We additionally show that this decomposition recovers functionally distinct subspaces within the same representation: on cue-conflict stimuli, shape-sensitive and texture-sensitive directions are largely non-overlapping, and ablating one selectively impairs the corresponding task while leaving the other intact. Task-sensitive subspaces also provide a principled foundation for continual learning: constraining gradient updates to lie orthogonal to the task-sensitive subspaces of prior tasks outperforms standard variance-based gradient projection methods. Finally, restricting representational comparisons to task-sensitive subspaces reveals that inter-model alignment is higher in task-sensitive directions than in task-insensitive directions at later layers, and that conditioning on different tasks over the same stimuli uncovers asymmetries that full-space similarity measures cannot detect. Together, these results establish that deep networks compute through a low-dimensional functional spine embedded within a much larger representational space.

cross BendGS: Observation-Conditioned Gaussian Self-Modeling for Continuum Robots

Authors: Jeong Woon Lee, Doungjun Kim, Namho Kim, Seungheon Chae, Jongwoo Kim, Hyoseok Hwang

Abstract: Visual self-models learn from images how a robot moves under motor commands. Continuum robots need them even more, as they bend continuously without measured joint angles or fixed link geometry. Existing continuum self-models predict point clouds, image-space curves, or future images, but not the next 3D shape, appearance, and tip pose together from an observed body. 3D Gaussians offer a renderable representation of shape and appearance, yet articulated Gaussian self-models rely on joint angles to drive deformation. In this work, we propose BendGS, which uses a virtual backbone estimated from observation to drive command-conditioned Gaussian deformation, enabling next-state prediction for continuum robots without measured joint angles. In Gaussian modeling, a feed-forward network reconstructs the robot as 3D Gaussians from multi-view images, and the backbone, a chain of coordinate frames, is fitted to back-projected depth points to estimate the bending state. In transition, a network predicts its change under a relative motor command to deform the bound Gaussians. The deformed Gaussians render the next images and the 3D shape, and the distal frame gives the tip pose. Training losses use RGB-D images and masks, without bending or tip labels. On simulated and real robots, BendGS achieves the lowest Chamfer distance, best image quality, and lowest tip errors among the compared methods.

cross Start Where the Data Are: Statistical Priors for One-Step Generation on Manifolds

Authors: Yuyao Wang, Haoliang Sun, Xiankai Lu, Xinxin Zhang, Yongshun Gong

Abstract: Fast generation on manifolds is important for scientific applications such as protein design, yet existing one-step generators often produce spurious samples in low-density gaps between data modes. Our analysis highlights the mismatch between uniform noise and concentrated data, motivating a statistical prior that captures the data's coarse structure. We first estimate this prior by fitting mixtures of tangent-space Gaussians through geometry-aware moment matching. However, independent noise-data pairing can still connect different modes and weaken the benefit of the fitted prior. We therefore introduce posterior coupling, which samples a latent component conditioned on each data point and draws its paired noise from that component. To extend this approach to long torsion-angle chains, where full-covariance mixtures become costly, we use a hidden Markov prior to model sequential dependence with shared parameters. The resulting framework plugs into existing one-step training pipelines while retaining the generator architecture, training objective, and single network evaluation at inference. Experiments on spheres, tori, and SO(3) demonstrate substantial improvements across multiple generators, reducing distributional error by up to $125\times$ and the protein Ramachandran outlier rate from 12.1% to 0.55%.

cross CALVIN-MAX: When Does a Mid-Execution Change Hurt a Robot Policy?

Authors: Sajjad Pakdamansavoji

Abstract: Robots must often keep working after the world changes, as when a block moves or the camera shifts while the robot still acts on what it saw before. Recent work showed that one change during a task lowers success for every policy tested, but did not separate the effect of its timing from the cost of the changed world, or measure what it costs afterwards. We introduce CALVIN-MAX, a benchmark of 7,828 cases on CALVIN's five-instruction chains, each with one change to the objects, the camera, the robot, or the instruction. Each case compares three runs that are identical until the affected instruction begins: one without the change, one with it in place from that moment, and one in which it arrives while the robot is moving. The second run against the first gives the cost of the changed world, and the third against the second the effect of meeting it mid-motion. On 1,526 held-out cases per policy, with hypotheses fixed in advance, each of ten released policies loses 4.4 to 9.9 percentage points on the affected instruction over the eight core changes. Whether timing matters depends on the change: one that alters what the robot sees costs 5.4 points less mid-motion than from the start, whereas a change to the scene itself or to the robot shows no detectable effect. Rewinding the robot to the start of the instruction removes the benefit for every policy; the stricter test we fixed in advance, that the rewound run then matches the second run, passes for three. The cost also outlasts the affected instruction: the change stays in place, and a policy that still completes that instruction is 5.0 to 16.8 points less likely to complete the next one.

cross Subliminal Learning Crosses Modalities

Authors: Saraswathy Amjith, Kimia Hamidieh, Antonio Torralba, Yoon Kim

Abstract: Subliminal learning refers to a surprising phenomenon in language models wherein a prompt given to one model (e.g., preference for cats) can be subliminally distilled to another model via training on data that is ostensibly meaningless (e.g., number sequences). This paper shows that text-based subliminal learning transfers cross-modally, in particular, a topic/preference (e.g., animals) transferred through number sequences across vision-language models also changes how images are generated and represented. First, we show that a diffusion model using the student as its text encoder generates the student's animal more often than with the original model as its text encoder, but not its color or style. We then investigate the mechanism behind this phenomenon and which concepts transfer across modalities. A constant component of the student's change accounts for much of the shift of image representations toward the trait. Adding this component uniformly can change the generated animal, which depends on the whole prompt, but not concepts such as color or style, which are encoded in a single token. Finally, we introduce weight lens as an approach to identify a subliminal trait from the weights of a single trained model, and show that it identifies the trait of nearly every student, including traits that do not surface in generated images.

cross IncrementDET: Continual AI-Generated Music Detection via Enrollment

Authors: Runbao Du, Wei Li, Qile He

Abstract: AI-generated music has become common on music streaming platforms, and listeners often cannot distinguish it from music made by humans. AI-music detection is therefore needed, but detectors trained on a fixed set of generators often fail on unseen generators. As new generators emerge frequently, updating a universal detector, a single model for all generators, typically requires substantial training data and computation, while risking performance degradation on previously learned generators. We therefore introduce IncrementDET, a detector that learns each new generator by a process called enrollment. For each generator, IncrementDET trains one linear classifier, which we call a needle, to separate samples of that generator from a fixed set of human reference tracks. Enrolling a new generator adds one needle while the previously constructed needles remain unchanged. A Sidak correction keeps the overall false-positive rate at its target as needles are added. To compare detectors under these conditions, we define the task of continual AI-generated music detection and introduce a benchmark in which generators arrive one at a time. The benchmark measures the accuracy of a detector before and after each update and the cost of each update. For this benchmark, we also construct EvoMusicFake-17K, a dataset of instrumental music from six recent generators and four human music platforms. Compared with SpecTTTra, a state-of-the-art universal detector, IncrementDET learns a new generator more accurately from only a few samples, generalizes better to unseen generators and better preserves detection performance on previously learned generators, at a substantially lower update cost. The structure of IncrementDET can also be applied to the detection of generated images and video, where new generators are released just as frequently.

cross Multi-view Random-set Learning

Authors: Mengchi Zuo, Tianyang Wang, Cai Xu, Ziyu Guan, Wei Zhao

Abstract: Trusted multi-view learning aims to integrate heterogeneous views for accurate and reliable predictions. However, multi-view data often exhibit the view degradation phenomenon: individual views contain only partial discriminative information and cannot independently determine the instance-level label. Requiring such views to produce unambiguous class evidence can distort evidence learning and mislead subsequent fusion. Existing methods addressing this problem have at least one of the following drawbacks: (1) assigning evidence to individual classes without explicitly representing unresolved class groups; (2) projecting composite beliefs onto individual classes before fusion, preventing direct refinement of candidate groups through cross-view set intersections. To address these limitations, we propose a new Multi-view Random-Set Learning (MRSL) paradigm, which preserves view-specific support for candidate class sets and refines candidate classes through cross-view fusion. Specifically, we quantify class overlap to select a compact collection of sets for each view, avoiding the exponential cost of modeling all class subsets. We then perform set-level fusion by assigning joint support to set intersections, allowing complementary views to narrow the candidate classes before probability conversion. Experiments on synthetic and eight real-world datasets validate the proposed overlap measure and demonstrate that MRSL achieves the highest accuracy on all eight datasets. Compared with the strongest baselines, MRSL improves average accuracy by 4.71% and reduces average expected calibration error by 6.33%. All datasets and code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

Abstract: Recovering articulated robot state from video supports metric spatial sensing for manipulation, scene reconstruction, and action planning. Prior task-specific regressors typically assume known intrinsics, fit one robot through laborious data collection and robot-specific training, and still transfer poorly across scenes. Updated tools and workflows are accepted only when the verifier judges a positive improvement. The retained workflow is frozen for accurate inference. cross Motion Chain of Thought: Disentangling Motion Semantics from Visual Appearance for Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Xiangkai Ma, Xinyi Wang, Junjie Wang, Sheng Xu, Mingyang Li, Yue Ma, Han Zhang, Yuzheng Zhuang, Wenzhong Li, Zhihao Yuan

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) aim to construct a unified architecture capable of understanding world state evolution and guiding action generation. However, existing visual branches focus on predicting static visual observation, rather than reflecting potential transition information that captures the evolution of world states under motion interactions. This leads to representational entanglement between high-level physical condition evolution and low-level action trajectory generation within the Action Model, creating a structural bottleneck while weakening the predictive capability of world evolution modeling for action generation. We propose PILOT (Physical Inference for Latent Optimized Trajectories), whose core Representational Deduction (RD) bridges this gap by integrating motion chain-of-thought (CoT) guidance as a native model capability. Specifically, RD aims to encourage the action branch to explicitly model potential state transition tokens, which are retained as CoT in the reasoning space to guide fine-grained motion trajectory. Experiments demonstrate that RD not only improves the success rate and generalization ability of WAMs in complex robotic manipulation tasks but also enhances the model's physical interpretability by decoupling high-level motion semantics from low-level trajectory details. Furthermore, the abundant state transition supervision signals introduced by RD effectively alleviate the sparse supervision in action generation, supporting data-efficient real-robot adaptation with reduced demonstration sets.

cross Characterizing Datasets to Anticipate Unsupervised Domain Adaptation Performance for Radiographic Pneumonia Detection

Authors: Iftakhar Hossain, Md. Imran Hasan, Salahuddin Ahmed, Eric D. McCollum, Abdullah H. Baqui, Taufiq Hasan

Abstract: Deep learning models for pneumonia detection from chest radiographs exhibit degraded accuracy when applied across institutions, which motivates Unsupervised Domain Adaptation (UDA). We observe that UDA performance depends on the properties of both the source and target datasets, and that performance gains are asymmetric with respect to dataset pairs. We investigate which structural properties of chest X-ray datasets are the most important predictors of UDA performance, and whether they can be measured before adaptation is attempted. Five chest radiograph datasets were characterized by the class separability of their features, obtained from one forward pass of a frozen DenseNet-121 and summarized by the Silhouette Score, with the Fisher Ratio as a supporting measure. All twenty directed source-target pairs were adapted with a Domain-Adversarial Neural Network (DANN), while direct transfer without adaptation was also evaluated. Seven published transferability estimators were computed on the same representation for comparison. We found that adaptation performance depended on both endpoints in opposite directions. More separable target datasets attained higher AUC scores ($r=0.934$, $p=0.042$), whereas more separable sources produced lower AUC scores ($r=-0.922$, $p=0.017$). The signed difference in separability tracked the signed difference in AUC between the two directions of a pair ($r=-0.932$, $p=0.017$) and identified the better direction in 10 of 10 pairs ($p=0.001$). The proposed descriptor was comparable to the best published estimators on target evaluation. Source and target datasets play opposite structural roles in adaptation: higher class separability makes a dataset easier as a test set but not as a training source. This framework can guide dataset selection for adaptation in low-resource settings without running full experiments.

cross See the Answer, Learn to Embed: Learning Universal Multimodal Embeddings from Hindsight

Authors: Yoonki Cho, Youngju Na, Taegeun Yang, Jinhwan Seo, Sung-Eui Yoon

Abstract: Universal Multimodal Embedding (UME) models map heterogeneous multimodal inputs into a shared embedding space, where pairwise similarity supports diverse downstream tasks. Recent reasoning-driven UME approaches use intermediate reasoning to generate additional context for embedding, but often incur extra computation at inference. In this paper, we explore the matched candidate (i.e., the paired target) as an alternative source of context, asking whether a model can "see the answer" during training and "learn to embed" without it at inference. However, direct conditioning on this privileged context can introduce candidate-specific information unavailable from the query alone or cause the candidate-conditioned query representation to collapse toward the candidate embedding. To address this, we introduce Hindsight-Enhanced Multimodal Embeddings (HEME), which learns query-grounded hindsight representations from such candidate context. HEME first trains a hindsight teacher with a hindsight attention mask, mismatched-candidate conditioning, and query-token alignment to limit excessive reliance on the candidate. It then freezes the teacher and distills its hindsight representation and similarity distribution into a standalone student embedder. At inference, the student independently encodes queries and candidates without candidate conditioning or the hindsight teacher. Experiments on MMEB-V2 and the agent and text tasks of MMEB-V3 demonstrate the effectiveness of HEME and show that it enables more efficient inference than explicit reasoning-driven methods.

cross ReFocus: Progress-Guided Adaptive Practice for Long-Horizon Robotic Manipulation Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Haoyuan Li, Wei Huang, Jiehong Lin, Shengrong Zhang, Chenzhi Liu, Shengchao Zhou, Hehe Fan, Xiaojuan Qi, Yi Yang

Abstract: Online reinforcement learning (RL) enables vision-language-action policies to improve through environmental interaction. However, in long-horizon manipulation, sparse success/failure feedback provides little information about where policies encounter bottlenecks. Moreover, repeated full-task rollouts also leave these bottlenecks weakly analyzed and insufficiently practiced. To this end, we introduce ReFocus, a progress-guided multi-round RL framework that alternates between Global and Local RL to identify task-specific bottlenecks and allocate additional practice to them. Specifically, during Global RL, we decompose each task into a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) of subtasks. Then a DAG-based Progress Reward is leveraged for both policy training and bottleneck diagnosis. Based on the identified bottlenecks, ReFocus selects Adaptive Practice Windows, defining task segments from an entry stage to a target stage. It allocates additional interaction to these segments during Local RL while updating the entire policy. After that, it evaluates the policy to perform the full task for the next round of RL, forming an iteration between Global and Local RL. Experiments on LIBERO-Long and RoboCasa365 show that ReFocus achieves success rates of 97.8% and 51.3%, respectively, compared with 90.6% and 39.3% for the $\pi_{0.5}$ baseline. Furthermore, we evaluate a real-world adaptation on three long-horizon tasks. On BreadBaker, ReFocus achieves 95% success, compared with 30% for the $\pi_{0.5}$. Project page: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.

URLs: https://refocus-robotics.github.io.

cross Cif-query bridging for audio-native asr transfer to visual speech recognition

Authors: Chang Sun, Dongliang Xie, Wanpeng Xie, Bo Qin, Hong Yang

Abstract: Audio-native automatic speech recognition (ASR) models provide strong speech-language priors, but their compact acoustic interfaces are mismatched with long, frame-level visual features. We propose CIF-Query Bridge, a lightweight connection from a Paraformer visual speech recognition (VSR) encoder to Qwen3-ASR. The existing visual continuous integrate-and-fire (CIF) predictor produces an input-dependent number of token-rate queries; a two-layer cross-attention bridge refines them against the complete frame sequence, and a linear adapter feeds the pretrained ASR projector and decoder. This design preserves access to uncompressed visual context while avoiding direct autoregressive processing of hundreds of video frames. Controlled ablations characterize bridge depth, projector initialization, adapter capacity, and CTC supervision. The results show that CIF-derived adaptive queries provide a compact interface for transferring audio-native ASR capability to VSR.

cross Seeing to Drive on a Single Board: Attention-Free Depth for Onboard Robot Navigation

Authors: Itai Mizlish, Boaz Ben-Moshe

Abstract: A small ground robot can carry one camera and a few watts of compute, but the depth foundation models that could turn that camera into a range sensor are vision transformers sized for GPUs. We bring one to a Raspberry Pi 5 with the Hailo-8L, an accelerator that runs every layer in integer arithmetic, attention included. Quantised for it, Depth Anything V2 loses large parts of the scene, and we measure why: at 449 tokens, 91% of softmax weights fall below one int8 step while carrying 17-33% of the attention mass. Our answer is to distil, not quantise. An attention-free ResNet-34 U-Net student of Depth Anything V2-Large (24.5M parameters) runs at 27.1 FPS on the chip, its int8 output matches its float twin (Pearson 0.99993), and on held-out scenes of its simulated yard it is the most accurate depth network on the chip (AbsRel 0.269 against 0.354-0.530). On 1,000 stereo-verified points of a real outdoor drive it is not resolvably different from the vendor's Depth Anything V2-Small build, while running faster (27.1 vs 21.6 FPS). Ranging obstacles by where they meet the floor needs only the camera height, so mapping, hybrid-A* planning and SLAM run on the Pi's own CPU. In real-time hardware-in-the-loop tests in a Gaussian-splat reconstruction of a real yard, the stack reaches the goal without contact in 7.9$\pm$1.1 of 10 scenes, against 9.2$\pm$0.4 with exact obstacles, and in 7.7$\pm$0.7 on its own pose with simulated wheel odometry (ten seeds each; 8.2$\pm$0.4 with the real Pi and Hailo-8L in the loop, five seeds). The source code, pre-trained models and data are available at https://github.com/itaim18/DANIEL.

cross Support2Sim: Visually Faithful Real2Sim with Stable Object Supports

Authors: Samarth Chopra, Ankit Prabhu, Akshayan Nagarajah, Sumer Murthy, Pratik Chaudhari, Vijay Kumar

Abstract: Reconstructing a scene for physics simulation requires recovering its spatial layout and the support relationships that govern its behavior. We present Support2Sim, a Real2Sim system that reconstructs simulation-ready scenes from a single RGB image. Our design objective is to maximize source fidelity, support realization, and stability within a practical time budget for scene generation. Support2Sim infers a support graph, which a contact-guided pose initializer uses to propose poses for objects with multiple supports. The initializer generates and ranks candidates by geometric fit and source-image agreement. A coarse-to-fine refiner allocates a fixed search budget across supported objects and evaluates pose updates with physics rollouts. After an accepted update, it searches from the new pose at a finer translation and rotation scale. We benchmark our method on 50 scenes spanning four datasets. Compared with the strongest baseline, Support2Sim improves source-mask IoU after 10 seconds by 21.4 pp, realizes 87.1% versus 73.2% of intended supports, and reduces mean object drift from 79.6 to 45.3 mm. cross Simulator-as-Proxy World Model for Robot Manipulation

Authors: Tingting Liao, Mingjia Huo, Hao Li, Zhengzhong Liu, Eric P. Xing, Guangyi Liu

Abstract: Video world models for robots are increasingly conditioned on a simulated or rendered proxy of the scene, on the assumption that the video will show what the proxy shows. Testing this assumption requires a proxy whose state is explicit and can be perturbed. We present SimPWM (Simulator-as-Proxy World Model): given a calibrated camera, it represents, from the first frame alone, the robot by its 3D model and the objects the gripper may touch as 3D points; a rule-based simulator with no trained parameters moves them under the actions, and a video model renders the result. SimPWM returns the realistic video together with the simulated state. We then freeze the simulated objects while the arm moves as recorded and check whether the video still shows the object lifted. On DROID episodes from unseen setups, the answer differs for the arm and for the objects. With each of three base video models, the arm follows the proxy (robot IoU 0.75-0.78, against at most 0.69 for end-to-end models); rendering the robot alone already gives this. What the simulated objects add is the object state: against a robot-only proxy, they cut missed pick-ups from 54% to 38%, and the video follows them only with some base models: on rigid objects, freezing them lowers the lift rate of MiniMax-H3, fine-tuned on episodes whose simulation agrees with the real video, from 42% to 10%, but that of LTX-2.3, trained on the same episodes, only from 57% to 49%, as if it inferred the grasp from the arm motion; more than half of the remaining lifts of MiniMax-H3 are of cloth and rope, which the simulator carries rigidly. With any base model, the simulated state records what the simulation did; under perturbed actions, a video that disagrees with it reveals that the video ignores the simulated state.

cross Residual Guidance for Real-Time Interactive Video Generation

Authors: NaHyeon Park, Kyeong-Hwan Moon, Youjin Jeon, Hyunjung Shim

Abstract: Real-time interactive video generation allows users to change the prompt during generation to steer the evolving video. However, existing streaming models, which generate videos chunk by chunk, often keep following the previous prompt after a switch. We trace this failure to context inertia, where the previous chunk acts as a strong prior that suppresses the new prompt. Furthermore, we find that this inertia persists even when the cache retains only the previous chunk, yet discarding the context breaks visual continuity, which limits approaches that edit the cache. Rather than editing the cache, we propose Residual Guidance (RG), a simple, training-free method that counteracts context inertia with a correction measured outside the cache. Specifically, RG denoises the re-noised previous chunk once under the new prompt without the cache and adds the resulting residual to the early denoising steps of the next chunk, where its content is largely decided and the new prompt is most suppressed. Across settings ranging from a single switch to long multi-switch generation, RG substantially improves prompt switching capability on existing streaming models, including those with dedicated switching mechanisms, while largely retaining visual quality. As it requires no training or per-model tuning and adds only one forward pass per chunk, RG can be easily integrated into existing real-time video generation pipelines.

cross AVQ-Attention-2: Adaptive Vector-Quantized Attention with Exact Local Blocks and Linear-Cost Training

Authors: Winfried van den Dool, Patrick Forr\'e, Amir Habibian, Yuki M. Asano, Max Welling

Abstract: Attention costs $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$ in the number of tokens. Vector-quantized attention reduces this to $\mathcal{O}(NM)$ by clustering the $N$ keys, attending to the $M$ codewords instead, and its adaptive form, AVQ-attention, concentrates codebook resolution where a query's attention mass actually falls. We present AVQ-Attention-2, a better design that merges it with exact local attention and attends to the mean of each cluster's keys rather than to a codeword. We show three improvements: consistently better quality at comparable cost; training that now also costs $\mathcal{O}(NM)$ rather than $\mathcal{O}(N^2)$; and an approximation accurate enough that the layer can be dropped into a pretrained transformer without fine-tuning. Across a variety of sequence lengths the result is competitive not merely with other approximate attention but with exact dense attention, running faster than FlashAttention at little to no loss in quality.

cross Read My Lips: Context-aware Lip Reading Using Language and Audio-Visual Models

Authors: Amir Razlighi, Zhuo Ning, Naomi Ken Korem, Nisarg Patel, Yiorgos Chrysanthou, Daniel Cohen-Or, Ali Mahdavi Amiri

Abstract: Real-world speech is often partially observable: audio may be missing or corrupted by noise and competing speakers, while the speaker's lips remain visible. Recovering speech requires combining what can be heard, seen, and inferred from context. We introduce RML (Read My Lips), a collaborative method combining a lip reader, a language model, and an audio-visual generative model. The lip reader provides articulation hypotheses, the language model uses context to resolve ambiguous or missing words, and the audio-visual generator reconstructs synchronized speech while preserving speaker identity. Since source reliability varies, RML generates multiple reconstructions and uses the available evidence to favor the most reliable ones. This allows RML to handle missing audio, strong interference, and poor visual evidence without manually defined thresholds. Quantitative and qualitative experiments across these conditions show the benefit of combining visual, acoustic, and linguistic signals.

cross iWAM: Efficient Action Prediction from World Models without Explicit Future Prediction

Authors: Vansh Kapoor, Max Sobol Mark, Sreyas Venkataraman, Max Simchowitz, Aviral Kumar

Abstract: World-action models (WAMs) build policies on pretrained video-generation models, on the premise that predicting how a scene evolves helps determine how to act. However, generating future scenes is expensive for real-time control. This motivates efficient proxies for future imagination, but how to train such proxies without sacrificing action-prediction quality remains unclear. An effective proxy need not generate a high-resolution frame; it only needs to provide enough information to distinguish between possible futures. This leads to the design principle behind iWAM: rather than denoising an entire future frame, can we condition the action policy on intermediate features from a single video-model evaluation? We show that the answer is yes. Exploiting the reversible dynamics of flow-matching models, we invert each training future into the corresponding latent noise vector. However, a single velocity evaluation at this latent does not necessarily yield informative features for action prediction. We therefore learn a bidirectional flow map that amortizes future-generation computation, enabling a single model evaluation to provide effective conditioning features. At inference, we sample a latent noise vector from the prior and condition the policy using just one video-backbone forward pass. Across simulated benchmarks and real robots, iWAM recovers the generalization benefits of WAMs while substantially reducing its inference cost.

cross ROAM-Bench: Evaluating Embodied Active Spatial Intelligence through Evidence Verification

Authors: Xingjian Ran, Xiaoye Mo, Sihao Liu, Li Luo, Taiqiang Wu, Ji Zhao, Wenqi Shao, Hongyang Li, Jiaqi Wang, Bo Dai

Abstract: Embodied spatial intelligence requires agents to gather information through exploration and interaction, then use it to reason and act. Existing benchmarks insufficiently distinguish correct outcomes from completion of these task requirements. We introduce ROAM-Bench to evaluate these abilities under robot motion and collision constraints. Independent checks use simulation state and episode records to assess outcomes and task requirements, with stronger checks of geometry, coverage, or placement. It comprises 250 manually validated tasks across 11 types in five groups and 27 indoor environments, covering object and room search, counting, geometric judgments, direction reasoning, and relocation. Human evaluators achieve 84% strict task success on 100 sampled tasks, with every correct outcome satisfying the required checks. Across seven frontier vision-language models, we identify two complementary failure modes: correct outcomes with unmet task requirements, and incorrect outcomes despite passing stronger checks. Oracle-input diagnostics show that more complete target observations and calibrated feedback improve final spatial judgments.

cross OmniAct: One Policy, One Million Embodiments

Authors: Xiang Fan, Yuheng Wang, Jiayuan Yan, Bohan Fang, Wisdom Oluchi Ikezogwo, Ainaz Eftekhar, Max Argus, Jason Ren, Jiafei Duan, Ranjay Krishna

Abstract: A single policy that controls diverse robots must not only transfer task knowledge across embodiments, but also adapt its behavior to each robot's capabilities. Existing approaches facilitate transfer through shared action representations, but these representations do not necessarily capture how differences in robot morphology should shape manipulation strategies. We introduce OmniAct, a cross-embodiment manipulation policy that conditions directly on each robot's kinematics in a specific scene to produce embodiment-specific behavior. This kinematic graph represents the robot's joint positions, motion axes, and connectivity to its visual observation, allowing the policy to account for how its embodiment related to objects and surfaces in the scene. A flow-matching policy predicts relative joint motions for each node, enabling control across robots with different degrees of freedom and kinematic configurations. To learn how physical structure shapes behavior at scale, we procedurally design and filter one million physically feasible robot embodiments; next, we develop algorithms to generate robot trajectories in simulation for all embodiments; finally, we train across diverse simulated manipulation tasks. On a previously unseen real-world robot, it achieves nearly twice the success rate of the next best method, MolmoAct2. Beyond task success, we observe that OmniAct learns distinct manipulation strategies that exploit the capabilities and accommodate the limitations of different morphologies. replace HindSight: A Graph-Based Vision Model Architecture For Representing Part-Whole Hierarchies

Authors: Muhammad AbdurRafae

Abstract: This paper presents a model architecture for encoding the representations of part-whole hierarchies in images in form of a graph. The idea is to divide the image into patches of different levels and then treat all of these patches as nodes for a fully connected graph. replace Retinexformer: One-stage Retinex-based Transformer for Low-light Image Enhancement

Authors: Yuanhao Cai, Hao Bian, Jing Lin, Haoqian Wang, Radu Timofte, Yulun Zhang

Abstract: When enhancing low-light images, many deep learning algorithms are based on the Retinex theory. However, the Retinex model does not consider the corruptions hidden in the dark or introduced by the light-up process. Besides, these methods usually require a tedious multi-stage training pipeline and rely on convolutional neural networks, showing limitations in capturing long-range dependencies. In this paper, we formulate a simple yet principled One-stage Retinex-based Framework (ORF). ORF first estimates the illumination information to light up the low-light image and then restores the corruption to produce the enhanced image. We design an Illumination-Guided Transformer (IGT) that utilizes illumination representations to direct the modeling of non-local interactions of regions with different lighting conditions. By plugging IGT into ORF, we obtain our algorithm, Retinexformer. Comprehensive quantitative and qualitative experiments demonstrate that our Retinexformer significantly outperforms state-of-the-art methods on thirteen benchmarks. The user study and application on low-light object detection also reveal the latent practical values of our method. Code, models, and results are available at https://github.com/caiyuanhao1998/Retinexformer . Our project page at https://caiyuanhao1998.github.io/project/Retinexformer/ shows more visual results. An online Gradio demo is available on Hugging Face at https://huggingface.co/spaces/CaiYuanhao/Retinexformer

replace Revisiting Out-of-Distribution Detection in Semantic Difference Space

Authors: Xingming Long, Jie Zhang, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen

Abstract: The primary goal of most existing out-of-distribution (OOD) detection works is to identify inputs with "Semantic Shifts". However, the existing definition of "Semantic Shift" in previous studies remains vague, resulting in controversial choices of OOD samples in many benchmarks. Consequently, some benchmarks are too trivial for evaluating OOD detection, whereas others include OOD instances that are practically undetectable. In this paper, we propose a formal definition of "Semantic Difference Space", which reflects the scope of a model's OOD detection capability after training convergence. The definition is grounded in an analysis of how the loss-based optimization shapes the model's perceptual toward OOD inputs, offering theoretical explanations for the controversies regarding the selection of OOD samples across existing benchmarks. To validate the correctness of our analysis, we construct multiple datasets, primarily in the context of image classification, and we find that compared to different OOD detection methods, different OOD settings play a more decisive role in the detection results. Finally, we propose a simple yet effective OOD Separability Probe (OSP) method to evaluate whether the existing OOD detection benchmarks are reasonable, identifying issues in some benchmarks and highlighting a few that are worth further exploration.

Abstract: Existing benchmarks often rely on predefined questions or reference captions, limiting their flexibility for evaluating detailed descriptions on new image collections. replace Persistence-based Line Detection in Hough Space

Authors: Johannes Ferner, Stefan Huber, Saverio Messineo, Angel Pop, Martin Uray

Abstract: The Hough transform is a popular and classical technique in computer vision for the detection of lines (or more general objects). It maps a pixel into a dual space -- the Hough space: each pixel is mapped to the set of lines through this pixel, which forms a curve in Hough space. The detection of lines then becomes a voting process to find those lines that received many votes by pixels. However, this voting is done by thresholding, which is susceptible to noise and other artifacts. In this work, we present an alternative voting technique to detect peaks in the Hough space based on persistent homology, which very naturally addresses limitations of simple thresholding. Experiments on synthetic data show that our method significantly outperforms the original method, while also demonstrating enhanced robustness. This work seeks to inspire future research in two key directions. First, we highlight the untapped potential of Topological Data Analysis techniques and advocate for their broader integration into existing methods, including well-established ones. Secondly, we initiate a discussion on the mathematical stability of the Hough transform, encouraging exploration of mathematically grounded improvements to enhance its robustness.

replace VFRTok: Variable Frame Rates Video Tokenizer with Duration-Proportional Information Assumption

Authors: Tianxiong Zhong, Xingye Tian, Boyuan Jiang, Xuebo Wang, Xin Tao, Pengfei Wan, Zhiwei Zhang

Abstract: Modern video generation frameworks based on Latent Diffusion Models suffer from inefficiencies in tokenization due to the Frame-Proportional Information Assumption. Existing tokenizers provide fixed temporal compression rates, causing the computational cost of the diffusion model to scale linearly with the frame rate. The paper proposes the Duration-Proportional Information Assumption: the upper bound on the information capacity of a video is proportional to the duration rather than the number of frames. Based on this insight, the paper introduces VFRTok, a Transformer-based video tokenizer, that enables variable frame rate encoding and decoding through asymmetric frame rate training between the encoder and decoder. Furthermore, the paper proposes Partial Rotary Position Embeddings (RoPE) to decouple position and content modeling, which groups correlated patches into unified tokens. The Partial RoPE effectively improves content-awareness, enhancing the video generation capability. Benefiting from the compact and continuous spatio-temporal representation, VFRTok achieves competitive reconstruction quality and state-of-the-art generation fidelity while using only 1/8 tokens compared to existing tokenizers. The code and weights are released at: https://github.com/KwaiVGI/VFRTok.

URLs: https://github.com/KwaiVGI/VFRTok.

replace AIR: Rethinking Image-Text Offset Alignment in Multimodal Contrastive Representation Space

Authors: Guimeng Liu, Milad Abdollahzadeh, Ngai-Man Cheung

Abstract: Image-text offset alignment, which refers to the consistency between visual transformation directions and their corresponding textual transformation directions, has become an important relational property in multimodal contrastive representation spaces such as CLIP and SigLIP. This property plays a central role in text-driven visual transformation tasks, including generative model domain adaptation and text-guided object-centric image editing, where textual offsets are used to guide visual transformations. These methods fundamentally rely on the assumption that image and text offsets are well aligned, such that textual transformation directions can provide reliable guidance for corresponding visual transformations. However, the validity of this alignment assumption has not been systematically examined. In this work, we question this foundational assumption by conducting a comprehensive empirical analysis of image-text offset alignment in multimodal contrastive representation space. Our findings reveal not only noticeable offset misalignment but also a meaningful positive correlation between image-text offset misalignment and semantic concept distance across six large datasets and eight contrastive vision-language models. Based on this discovery, we propose Adaptation with Iterative Refinement (AIR), a method that iteratively refines text offsets through anchor sampling and our proposed concept description learning to mitigate the downstream effect of image-text offset misalignment and improve guidance accuracy. Comprehensive experiments on zero-shot generative model domain adaptation and text-guided object-centric image editing across 40 setups, including qualitative, quantitative, and user studies, consistently show that AIR enables state-of-the-art performance in these tasks.

replace ZIP: Scalable Crowd Counting via Zero-Inflated Poisson Modeling

Authors: Yiming Ma, Victor Sanchez, Tanaya Guha

Abstract: The majority of crowd counting methods directly regress blockwise density maps using losses based on Mean Squared Error (MSE). This practice has two critical limitations: 1) it fails to account for the extreme spatial sparsity of annotations (i.e., over 95% of standard $8\times8$ blocks are empty across most benchmarks), which makes supervision signals in informative regions diluted by the predominant zeros. 2) MSE corresponds to a Gaussian error model that poorly matches discrete, non-negative count data. We introduce a novel crowd counting framework that is scalable and directly addresses the above limitations. This framework, named ZIP, models blockwise counts with a Zero-Inflated Poisson likelihood: A zero-inflation term models excess zeros in sparse annotations, while the Poisson component captures expected counts while respecting their discrete, non-negative nature. We provide a generalization analysis showing that the ZIP negative log-likelihood (NLL) has the same order of uniform generalization gap as Poisson NLL while offering a richer zero-inflated likelihood family, and that its bound scales more favorably with the number of spatial blocks than optimal-transport-based supervision. To assess the scalability and broad applicability of ZIP, we apply the same formulation across diverse backbones, from lightweight models to high-capacity architectures, spanning more than $100\times$ in both parameter count and inference FLOPs. Experiments on four standard crowd-counting benchmarks show that ZIP achieves state-of-the-art or highly competitive performance across a wide range of backbone capacities. Although ZIP is designed for sparse blockwise count maps, it also remains effective in dense crowd scenes, reducing MAE and RMSE by 3.9% and 8.5%, respectively, over mPrompt on UCF-QNRF. Code and trained weights are available at https://github.com/Yiming-M/ZIP.

URLs: https://github.com/Yiming-M/ZIP.

replace A2Mamba: Attention-augmented State Space Models for Visual Recognition

Authors: Meng Lou, Yunxiang Fu, Yizhou Yu

Abstract: Transformers and Mamba, initially invented for natural language processing, have inspired backbone architectures for visual recognition. Recent studies integrated Local Attention Transformers with Mamba to capture both local details and global contexts. Despite competitive performance, these methods are limited to simple stacking of Transformer and Mamba layers without any interaction mechanism between them. Thus, deep integration between Transformer and Mamba layers remains an open problem. We address this problem by proposing A2Mamba, a powerful Transformer-Mamba hybrid network architecture, featuring a new token mixer termed Multi-scale Attention-augmented State Space Model (MASS), where multi-scale attention maps are integrated into an attention-augmented SSM (A2SSM). A key step of A2SSM performs a variant of cross-attention by spatially aggregating the SSM's hidden states using the multi-scale attention maps, which enhances spatial dependencies pertaining to a two-dimensional space while improving the dynamic modeling capabilities of SSMs. Our A2Mamba outperforms all previous ConvNet-, Transformer-, and Mamba-based architectures in visual recognition tasks. For instance, A2Mamba-L achieves an impressive 86.1% top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K. In semantic segmentation, A2Mamba-B exceeds CAFormer-S36 by 2.5% in mIoU, while exhibiting higher efficiency. In object detection and instance segmentation with Cascade Mask R-CNN, A2Mamba-S surpasses MambaVision-B by 1.2%/0.9% in AP^b/AP^m, while having 40% fewer parameters. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/LMMMEng/A2Mamba.

URLs: https://github.com/LMMMEng/A2Mamba.

replace Sample-Aware Test-Time Adaptation for Medical Image-to-Image Translation

Authors: Irene Iele, Francesco Di Feola, Matteo Tortora, Rosa Sicilia, Valerio Guarrasi, Paolo Soda

Abstract: Image-to-image translation has emerged as a powerful technique in medical imaging, enabling tasks such as image denoising and cross-modality conversion. However, it suffers from limitations in handling out-of-distribution samples without causing performance degradation. To address this limitation, we propose a novel Test-Time Adaptation (TTA) framework that dynamically adjusts the translation process based on the characteristics of each test sample. Our method introduces a Reconstruction Module to quantify the domain shift and a Dynamic Adaptation Block that selectively modifies the internal features of a pretrained translation model to mitigate the shift without compromising the performance on in-distribution samples that do not require adaptation. We evaluate our approach on two medical image-to-image translation tasks: low-dose CT denoising and T1 to T2 MRI translation, showing consistent improvements over both the baseline translation model without TTA and prior TTA methods. Our analysis highlights the limitations of the state-of-the-art that uniformly apply the adaptation to both out-of-distribution and in-distribution samples, demonstrating that dynamic, sample-specific adjustment offers a promising path to improve model resilience in real-world scenarios. The code is available at: https://github.com/Sample-Aware-TTA/Code.

URLs: https://github.com/Sample-Aware-TTA/Code.

replace TimeMachine: Fine-Grained Facial Age Editing with Identity Preservation

Authors: Yilin Mi, Qixin Yan, Zheng-Peng Duan, Chunle Guo, Hubery Yin, Hao Liu, Chen Li, Jing Lyu, Chongyi Li

Abstract: With the advancement of generative models, facial image editing has made significant progress. However, achieving fine-grained age editing while preserving personal identity remains a challenging task. In this paper, we propose TimeMachine, a unified diffusion-based framework for accurate facial age editing with identity preservation. TimeMachine employs fine-grained age conditions and a Multi-Cross-Attention module to decouple the dedicated age and identity conditions. This branch-wise design enables controllable age transformation while maintaining identity consistency. Furthermore, we introduce an Age Classifier Guidance (ACG) module that predicts age embeddings from latent denoising features without reconstructing images during training. This lightweight training-only predictor provides additional age-related supervision and is removed during inference. To support large-scale training, we construct HFFA (High-quality Fine-grained Facial-Age), a million-scale collection of high-resolution face images annotated with captions, identity embeddings, MiVOLO-estimated numerical ages, and age embeddings. Extensive experiments across five public face datasets demonstrate that TimeMachine achieves superior age accuracy and identity preservation relative to the evaluated baselines, with consistent conclusions across five age estimators.

Authors: Yuzhi Lai, Shenghai Yuan, Peizheng Li, Jun Lou, Zhouyu Ran, Andreas Zell

Abstract: We present SEER-VAR, a novel framework for egocentric vehicle-based augmented reality (AR) that unifies semantic decomposition, Context-Aware SLAM Branches (CASB), and LLM-driven recommendation. Unlike existing systems that assume static or single-view settings, SEER-VAR dynamically separates cabin and road scenes via depth-guided vision-language grounding. Two SLAM branches track egocentric motion in each context, while a GPT-based module generates context-aware overlays such as dashboard cues and hazard alerts. To support evaluation, we introduce EgoSLAM-Drive, a real-world dataset featuring synchronized egocentric views, 6DoF ground-truth poses, and AR annotations across diverse driving scenarios. Experiments demonstrate that SEER-VAR achieves robust spatial alignment and perceptually coherent AR rendering across varied environments. As one of the first to explore LLM-based AR recommendation in egocentric driving, we address the lack of comparable systems through structured prompting and detailed user studies. Results show that SEER-VAR enhances perceived scene understanding, overlay relevance, and driver ease, providing an effective foundation for future research in this direction. Code and dataset will be made open source.

replace Concepts in Motion: Temporal Concept Bottleneck Model for Interpretable Video Classification

Authors: Patrick Knab, Sascha Marton, Philipp J. Schubert, Drago Guggiana, Christian Bartelt

Abstract: Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) enable interpretable image classification by structuring predictions around human-understandable concepts, but extending this paradigm to video remains challenging due to the difficulty of extracting concepts and modeling them over time. In this paper, we introduce MoTIF (Moving Temporal Interpretable Framework), a transformer-based concept architecture that operates on sequences of temporally grounded concept activations, by employing per-concept temporal self-attention to model when individual concepts recur and how their temporal patterns contribute to predictions. Central to the framework is a class-conditioned VLM-based concept discovery module that extracts object- and action-centric textual concepts from training videos, yielding temporally expressive concept sets without manual concept annotation. Across multiple video benchmarks, this combination improves over global concept bottlenecks and remains competitive within the interpretable concept-bottleneck setting, while narrowing the gap to strong black-box video baselines that we report as contextual references. Code available at github.com/patrick-knab/MoTIF.

replace Rethinking Multiplication in Neural Networks with Hadamard Transform-Assisted Operators

Authors: Emadeldeen Hamdan, Marouane Tliba, Ulas Bagci, Ahmet Enis Cetin

Abstract: Multiplication-avoiding neural operators are attractive for compute-in-memory (CIM) architectures, but directly replacing conventional convolutions often causes accuracy degradation. We investigate whether this limitation arises from multiplication avoidance itself or from the representation on which the operator acts. In this work, we introduce Hadamard Transform-assisted multiplication-avoiding (HMA) operators that combine Walsh--Hadamard transform features with multiplication-avoiding channel mixing. To identify where conventional multiplication remains necessary, we employ a gated formulation in which conventional and HMA operators compete through learned layer-wise coefficients. ResNet-18 experiments show that intermediate convolutional layers assign greater weight to HMA, whereas the earliest layers and final classifier retain greater contribution from conventional computation. Guided by this observation, we construct a compact hybrid HMA network and train it using knowledge distillation from a conventional teacher. On CIFAR-10, the resulting ResNet-18 student achieves 91.2\% accuracy compared with 90.4\% for the corresponding conventional baseline while reducing parameters by 68.25\%, conventional MACs by 85.71\%, and general multiplications by 85.54\%. Across ResNet-18, ResNet-50, and MobileNetV2 on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Tiny-ImageNet, HMA with knowledge distillation matches or improves the conventional models. These results show that Hadamard-domain feature restructuring can improve the accuracy--complexity tradeoff of multiplication-avoiding networks and provide a practical path toward efficient CIM inference.

replace FlowLUT: Efficient Image Enhancement via Differentiable LUTs and Iterative Flow Matching

Authors: Liubing Hu, Chen Wu, Anrui Wang, Dianjie Lu, Guijuan Zhang, Zhuoran Zheng

Abstract: Deep learning-based image enhancement methods face a fundamental trade-off between computational efficiency and representational capacity. For example, although a conventional three-dimensional Look-Up Table (3D LUT) can process a degraded image in real time, it lacks representational flexibility and depends solely on a fixed prior. To address this problem, we introduce FlowLUT, a novel end-to-end model that integrates the efficiency of LUTs, multiple priors, and the parameter-independent characteristic of flow-matched reconstructed images. Specifically, firstly, the input image is transformed in color space by a collection of differentiable 3D LUTs (containing a large number of 3D LUTs with different priors). Subsequently, a lightweight content-aware dynamically predicts fusion weights, enabling scene-adaptive color correction with $\mathcal{O}(1)$ complexity. Next, a lightweight fusion prediction network runs on multiple 3D LUTs, with $\mathcal{O}(1)$ complexity for scene-adaptive color correction.Furthermore, to address the inherent representation limitations of LUTs, we design an innovative iterative flow matching method to restore local structural details and eliminate artifacts. Finally, the entire model is jointly optimized under a composite loss function enforcing perceptual and structural fidelity. Extensive experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of our method on three benchmarks.

replace Fine-Grained Caching for Diffusion Transformers with Few Calibration Conditions

Authors: Zihao Wu, Bohan Zeng, Yuanxing Zhang

Abstract: Diffusion transformers require repeated denoiser evaluations, making image and video generation computationally expensive. We propose a training-free framework that uses a few calibration conditions to construct a fixed, fine-grained module-reuse schedule without schedule search. The design is motivated by an empirical effect along deterministic sampling trajectories: with initial noise fixed, condition-dependent deviations in several module outputs evolve similarly across adjacent steps, so temporal differencing attenuates much of their variation. We term this effect Conditional Common-Mode Rejection (CCMR). Motivated by this observation, we rank timestep--layer--module locations using normalized ratios of consecutive feature displacements and construct a Cache Book through two-stage calibration. The second stage updates a scorer-only reference with each provisional bit after recording its indexed score, while retaining full-compute trajectories. At inference, the fixed schedule requires no per-input policy estimation and can be nested within fixed step-wise cache policies. Module caching alone achieves $1.66$--$1.72\times$ speedup on the evaluated image models and $1.54$--$1.68\times$ on the video models. Combining it with MagCache or SeaCache provides further acceleration at a model-dependent cost in paired fidelity.

replace EditThinker: Enhancing Image Editors via Iterative Reasoning with a Plug-and-Play VLM

Authors: Hongyu Li, Manyuan Zhang, Dian Zheng, Ziyu Guo, Yimeng Jia, Kaituo Feng, Hao Yu, Yexin Liu, Yan Feng, Peng Pei, Xunliang Cai, Linjiang Huang, Hongsheng Li, Si Liu

Abstract: Image editors can produce realistic images while still failing to satisfy the requested changes. Improving their instruction following requires identifying these failures and translating visual feedback into instructions that the editor can execute. We introduce EditThinker, an 8B vision-language model (VLM) that enhances existing image editors through iterative reasoning while keeping their weights fixed. EditThinker jointly critiques semantic consistency and visual quality, reasons about editing failures, and refines the instruction in a structured response. It interacts with the editor through a Critique-Refine-Repeat loop, providing a plug-and-play interface for feedback-driven editing. Learning effective refinements requires more than imitating plausible expert suggestions: the instructions must work with the capabilities of the underlying editor. We therefore combine supervised fine-tuning on expert demonstrations with reinforcement learning from executed editing outcomes, using format, critic, and edit rewards. To support this training, we construct ThinkEdit-167k, a dataset of 167k step-wise samples with unified supervision for critique, reasoning, and instruction refinement. Across four tested editors, EditThinker improves overall scores on GEdit-Bench-EN, ImgEdit-Bench, RISE-Bench, and KRIS-Bench. Ablations examine the joint model design, data allocation, and training across VLM backbones. In our GEdit round-budget study, refinement achieves higher scores with fewer editor calls than best-of-N selection. We will release our data construction framework, datasets, and models.

replace Modular Neural Image Signal Processing

Authors: Mahmoud Afifi, Zhongling Wang, Ran Zhang, Michael S. Brown

Abstract: Neural image signal processing (ISP) pipelines have largely abandoned the modular design of classical ISPs in favor of black-box networks, gaining expressiveness but losing interpretability, controllability, and the ability to swap or extend stages. Reintroducing modularity exposes a tradeoff: pipelines built from fixed operators or fixed neural proxies preserve interpretability but limit expressiveness, while jointly optimizing the proxies recovers expressiveness but often entangles functional roles across stages. We present a practical role-preserving and yet expressive neural ISP built on a two-level training strategy. At the module level, components with available ground truth are optimized separately to preserve functional decomposition. At the operator level, where intermediate ground truth is impractical to obtain, operators are trained jointly under final-image supervision but kept aligned with their roles via architectural design and role specific losses. The resulting framework is simultaneously interpretable, controllable, and expressive, enabling operator-level adjustment, targeted debugging, multi-style extension without full-pipeline retraining, and rendering of raw images from unseen cameras by swapping the camera specific modules. Lightweight at 0.5-3.9M parameters, our ISP achieves state-of-the-art results on most S24 picture styles and competitive performance on MIT-Adobe FiveK at a fraction of the parameter count of the closest neural ISPs. Code and the photo-editing tool are available at https://github.com/mahmoudnafifi/modular_neural_isp.

URLs: https://github.com/mahmoudnafifi/modular_neural_isp.

replace Beyond Primitive Bloat: High-Fidelity Gaussian Splatting via Coordinate-Convolution Neural Network

Authors: Abhinav Kumar, Tristan Aumentado-Armstrong, Lazar Valkov, Gopal Sharma, Alex Levinshtein, Radek Grzeszczuk, Suren Kumar

Abstract: Gaussian Splatting (GS) has revolutionized Novel View Synthesis (NVS) with fast training and real-time rendering, yet pushing its fidelity to arbitrary high levels remains an open challenge. We theoretically explain this barrier by proving that discrete Gaussian representations require an exponentially increasing number of primitives to raise training PSNR, which inevitably triggers optimization issues and validation overfitting. To bypass this scaling limit without primitive bloat, we formulate detail recovery as a continuous neural field over coarse renderings and prove that monotonically reducing approximation risk to zero requires jointly leveraging low-fidelity signals, local neighborhood, and coordinate queries. Guided by this result, we augment splatted renderings with an existing Coordinate-Convolution Neural Network (CCNN) that jointly convolves rendered low-fidelity signals with 2D pixel coordinate queries to synthesize residuals. Integrating CCNN into five diverse Gaussian Splatting baselines establishes new state-of-the-art fidelity across NVS benchmarks while preserving real-time rendering speeds with minimal training and storage overhead. Beyond NVS, they also consistently boost performance of 1D/2D regression and 2D super-resolution tasks.

replace Taming Hallucinations: Boosting MLLMs' Video Understanding via Counterfactual Video Generation

Authors: Zhe Huang, Hao Wen, Aiming Hao, Bingze Song, Meiqi Wu, Jiahong Wu, Xiangxiang Chu, Sheng Lu, Haoqian Wang

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made remarkable progress in video understanding. However, they suffer from a critical vulnerability: an over-reliance on language priors, which can lead to visual ungrounded hallucinations, especially when processing counterfactual videos that defy common sense. This limitation, stemming from the intrinsic data imbalance between text and video, is challenging to address due to the substantial cost of collecting and annotating counterfactual data. To address this, we introduce DualityForge, a novel counterfactual data synthesis framework that employs controllable, diffusion-based video editing to transform real-world videos into counterfactual scenarios. By embedding structured contextual information into the video editing and QA generation processes, the framework automatically produces high-quality QA pairs together with original-edited video pairs for contrastive training. Based on this, we build DualityVidQA, a large-scale video dataset designed to reduce MLLM hallucinations. In addition, to fully exploit the contrastive nature of our paired data, we propose Duality-Normalized Advantage Training (DNA-Train), a two-stage SFT-RL training regime where the RL phase applies pair-wise $\ell_1$ advantage normalization, thereby enabling a more stable and efficient policy optimization. Experiments on DualityVidQA-Test demonstrate that our method substantially reduces model hallucinations on counterfactual videos, yielding a relative improvement of 24.0% over the Qwen2.5-VL-7B baseline. Moreover, our approach achieves significant gains across both hallucination and general-purpose benchmarks, indicating strong generalization capability. We will open-source our dataset and code.

replace On the Role of Rotation Equivariance in Monocular 2D-to-3D Human Pose Lifting

Authors: Pavlo Melnyk, Cuong Le, Urs Waldmann, Per-Erik Forss\'en, Bastian Wandt

Abstract: We consider monocular 3D human pose estimation (HPE), focusing on lifting detected 2D keypoints to 3D skeletal joints. Despite their success, we find that commonly used lifting models, including a state-of-the-art probabilistic method, exhibit substantial performance degradation under in-plane rotations. We systematically study SO(2) rotation equivariance and robustness across lifting models, comparing approaches that learn approximate equivariance via data augmentation with those that enforce exact equivariance via architectural constraints or pose canonicalisation. We investigate their effects on prediction accuracy, rotational robustness, cross-dataset generalisation, and computational efficiency. Using established HPE benchmarks, we show that rotation-based augmentation applied jointly to input and output poses substantially improves accuracy on rotated poses, reducing error by up to 72\% with little loss of original-pose accuracy. These improvements also extend to probabilistic lifting and real-world sequences containing natural full-body rotations. While augmented models can outperform specialised equivariant architectures at considerably lower inference cost, comparisons using canonicalisation-based versions of the same lifting models demonstrate that exact equivariance need not compromise prediction accuracy. Direct equivariance diagnostics further show that rotational robustness does not imply exact equivariance. Overall, the relative benefits of learning or enforcing equivariance depend on the lifting backbone and the mechanism used to achieve it.

replace Where is the multimodal goal post? On the Ability of Foundation Models to Recognize Highlights in Videos

Authors: Aditya K Surikuchi, Raquel Fern\'andez, Sandro Pezzelle

Abstract: Foundation models are used for many real-world applications involving language understanding and generation from temporally-ordered multimodal events. Specifically, we focus on football games and evaluate models on their ability to distinguish between important and non-important sub-events in a game. Our findings underline the importance of modular architectures that dynamically adapt to multimodal heterogeneity and the need for complementary training procedures that maximize cross-modal synergy.

Abstract: Streaming reconstruction from monocular image sequences remains challenging, as existing methods typically favor either high-quality rendering or accurate geometry, but rarely both. Authors: Shuyang Wu, Yifu Qiu, John-Melle Bokhorst, Ines P Nearchou, Sandrine Prost, Jonathan A Fallowfield, Hakan Bilen, Timothy J Kendall

Abstract: Pathologists make a low power assessment followed by limited but targeted higher power evaluation of regions of interest as the standard approach in gold-standard clinical histopathological reporting. Multiple-instance Learning (MIL) is commonly used for slide-level predictions in computational pathology and approaches using multi-scale features have been developed. However, existing multi-scale MIL methods typically rely on inflexible multi-magnification inputs and computationally expensive architectures. We therefore conceptualise a more efficient MIL method that is explicitly designed to mimic the efficient gold-standard human pathologist approach to multi-scale assessment. In this paper, we propose the Multi-scale Pyramidal Network (MSPN), a generalisable module for attention-based MIL. MSPN introduces progressive multi-scale whole-slide image analysis using only a single high-magnification input. It consists of (1) grid-based remapping that aggregates high-magnification features to derive spatially-aware coarse feature maps, and (2) the Coarse Guidance Network (CGN) that learns coarse contexts. We take MSPN as a generalisable module to 4 attention-based frameworks and benchmark on 11 clinically relevant tasks across 10 publicly available datasets with 2 foundation models. Our results demonstrate that by modelling the human pathologist paradigm in slide evaluation, MSPN consistently improves MIL across the compared configurations and is competitive on performance against 8 state-of-the-art multi-scale models while remaining lightweight and accessible in a manner that may increase implementability into under-resourced clinical systems.

replace Self-Supervised Uncalibrated Multi-View Video Anonymization in the Operating Room

Authors: Keqi Chen, Vinkle Srivastav, Armine Vardazaryan, Cindy Rolland, Didier Mutter, Nicolas Padoy

Abstract: Privacy preservation is a prerequisite for using video data in Operating Room (OR) research. Effective anonymization relies on the exhaustive localization of every individual; even a single missed detection necessitates extensive manual correction. However, existing approaches face two critical scalability bottlenecks: (1) they usually require manual annotations of each new clinical site for high accuracy; (2) while multi-camera setups have been widely adopted to address single-view ambiguity, camera calibration is typically required whenever cameras are repositioned. To address these problems, we propose a self-supervised multi-view video anonymization framework consisting of whole-body person detection and whole-body pose estimation, without annotation or camera calibration. Our core strategy is to enhance the single-view detector by "retrieving" false negatives using temporal and multi-view context, and conducting self-supervised domain adaptation. We first run an off-the-shelf whole-body person detector in each view with a low-score threshold to gather candidate detections. Then, we retrieve the low-score false negatives that exhibit consistency with the high-score detections via tracking and self-supervised uncalibrated multi-view association. These recovered detections serve as pseudo labels to iteratively fine-tune the whole-body detector. Finally, we apply whole-body pose estimation on each detected person, and fine-tune the pose model using its own high-score predictions. Experiments on the 4D-OR dataset of simulated surgeries and our dataset of real surgeries show the effectiveness of our approach achieving 99% and 97% recall, respectively. Moreover, we train a real-time whole-body detector using our pseudo labels, achieving comparable performance and highlighting our method's practical applicability. The code is available at https://github.com/CAMMA-public/OR_anonymization.

URLs: https://github.com/CAMMA-public/OR_anonymization.

replace SparVAR: Exploring Sparsity in Visual AutoRegressive Modeling for Training-Free Acceleration

Authors: Zekun Li, Ning Wang, Tongxin Bai, Changwang Mei, Peisong Wang, Shuang Qiu, Jian Cheng

Abstract: Visual AutoRegressive (VAR) modeling has garnered significant attention for its innovative next-scale prediction paradigm. However, mainstream VAR paradigms attend to all tokens across historical scales at each autoregressive step. As the next scale resolution grows, the computational complexity of attention increases quartically with resolution, causing substantial latency. Prior accelerations often skip high-resolution scales, which speeds up inference but discards high-frequency details and harms image quality. To address these problems, we present SparVAR, a training-free acceleration framework that exploits three properties of VAR attention: (i) strong attention sinks, (ii) cross-scale activation similarity, and (iii) pronounced locality. Specifically, we dynamically predict the sparse attention pattern of later high-resolution scales from a sparse decision scale, and construct scale self-similar sparse attention via an efficient index-mapping mechanism, enabling high-efficiency sparse attention computation at large scales. Furthermore, we propose cross-scale local sparse attention and implement an efficient block-wise sparse kernel, which achieves $\mathbf{> 5\times}$ faster forward speed than FlashAttention. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the proposed SparVAR can reduce the generation time of an 8B model producing $1024\times1024$ high-resolution images to the 1s, without skipping the last scales. Compared with the VAR baseline accelerated by FlashAttention, our method achieves a $\mathbf{1.57\times}$ speed-up while preserving almost all high-frequency details. When combined with existing scale-skipping strategies, SparVAR attains up to a $\mathbf{2.28\times}$ acceleration, while maintaining competitive visual generation quality. Code is available at https://github.com/CAS-CLab/SparVAR

URLs: https://github.com/CAS-CLab/SparVAR

Authors: Manuel Barusco, Davide Dalle Pezze, Francesco Borsatti, Gian Antonio Susto

Abstract: Visual Anomaly Detection (VAD) is crucial for industrial inspection. However, most methods are designed for single-category models, and Teacher-Student (TS) approaches, despite their efficiency, have not been designed for the Continual scenario, where they need a replay buffer. To bridge this gap, we propose AdapTS, a unified TS framework for multi-class and continual settings, optimized for resource-constrained deployment. A single shared frozen backbone acts as teacher and student, and lightweight trainable adapters, selected at inference by a prototype-based task identification (99% accuracy), store the knowledge of each category without keeping past data. AdapTS is thus a viable solution for resource-constrained deployment.

replace Think, Act, Build: An Agentic Framework with Vision Language Models for Zero-Shot 3D Visual Grounding

Authors: Haibo Wang, Zihao Lin, Zhiyang Xu, Lifu Huang

Abstract: 3D Visual Grounding (3D-VG) aims to localize objects in 3D scenes via natural language descriptions. While recent advancements leveraging Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have explored zero-shot possibilities, they typically suffer from a static workflow relying on preprocessed 3D point clouds, essentially degrading grounding into proposal matching. To bypass this reliance, our core motivation is to decouple the task: leveraging 2D VLMs to resolve complex spatial semantics, while relying on deterministic multi-view geometry to instantiate the 3D structure. Driven by this insight, we propose "Think, Act, Build (TAB)", a dynamic agentic framework that reformulates 3D-VG tasks as a generative 2D-to-3D reconstruction paradigm operating directly on raw RGB-D streams. Specifically, guided by a specialized 3D-VG skill, our VLM agent dynamically invokes visual tools to track and reconstruct the target across 2D frames. Crucially, to overcome the multi-view coverage deficit caused by strict VLM semantic tracking, we introduce the Semantic-Anchored Geometric Expansion, a mechanism that first anchors the target in a reference video clip and then leverages multi-view geometry to propagate its spatial location across unobserved frames. This enables the agent to "Build" the target's 3D representation by aggregating these multi-view features via camera parameters, directly mapping 2D visual cues to 3D coordinates. Furthermore, to ensure rigorous assessment, we identify flaws such as reference ambiguity and category errors in existing benchmarks and manually refine the incorrect queries.
