replace VMSA: Video Memory Skill Agent for Long Video Understanding

Authors: Changin Choi, Jungwon Park, Jungmin Ko, Jimyeong Kim, Wonjong Rhee

Abstract: Long-video question answering requires retrieving and integrating relevant evidence from videos spanning hours or even days. Recent agentic approaches organize video information into external memories and iteratively retrieve evidence to answer questions. However, effective retrieval and reasoning remain challenging, as agents must determine what information to search for, which memory sources to access, and how to connect evidence across distant temporal intervals. To address these challenges, we propose Video Memory Skill Agent (VMSA), a framework that jointly designs structured video memories and memory skills for long-video understanding. VMSA constructs three complementary memory components. Segment Caption Memory and Entity Event Memory serve as searchable evidence sources, while Entity-Aware Episode Memory provides broad yet detailed video context to guide evidence retrieval and reasoning. To further support effective memory utilization, VMSA induces memory skills from successful agent trajectories, incrementally refining or extending a compact skill library to guide iterative evidence retrieval, reasoning, and verification. Across seven long-video question-answering benchmarks, VMSA achieves the highest accuracy on all three week-long egocentric benchmarks, outperforming the strongest baseline by up to 9.4% on Ego-R1 Bench, and the best or competitive performance on four additional benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of all three memory components and memory skills, with memory skills also improving temporal evidence localization.

replace Self-Supervised Continuous-Time Depth Learning and Ego-Motion Estimation from Asynchronous Events

Authors: Sadiq O. Macaulay, Dipon Kumar Ghosh, Hadfield Simon

Abstract: Event cameras register brightness changes pixel by pixel with microsecond precision, whereas the dense depth that could supervise them is costly to acquire and exists only at isolated instants. Condensing the stream into frames eases learning, but at the expense of the very timing that sets event sensing apart. We propose a self-supervised approach in which each event is assigned its own depth in continuous time, and the camera motion is recovered from the same observations. Supervision arises from geometry: lifted into 3D by its predicted depth and displaced by the camera motion at the instant it fired, an event should coincide with others produced by the same edge, so any misalignment exposes an error in depth or motion. Differentiable rendering then turns these per-event estimates into dense depth maps at target times within each window, faithful to the events behind them. On MVSEC, the approach outperforms prior event-only self-supervised methods in most sequences and depth ranges, with the widest margins at night, and recovers camera rotation more accurately than an earlier unsupervised event-based method. Ablations isolate the roles of local spatiotemporal context, the temporal backbone, and the alignment objective. These results position motion-compensated event alignment as a principled source of supervision for continuous-time depth and ego-motion estimation.

replace TaskVICL: Task-Conditioned Decoder States for Visual In-Context Learning

Authors: Chenyang Wang, Jiamo Hu, Chengrui Wu, Lingyue Chen, Jianguo Chen, Xun Xu, Xulei Yang

Abstract: The usefulness of an in-context demonstration for a vision-language model (VLM) depends on both the query image and the task posed about it. Common retrievers rank examples by image similarity and ignore the task, while task-aware alternatives require answer generation, external encoders or retriever training. We ask whether the frozen target VLM's own decoder states, read before it answers, can select demonstrations directly. TaskVICL represents each image and task by a task-conditioned processing signature: the hidden states at the final input position across all decoder layers. Its training-free variant, TaskVICL-Raw, averages cosine similarities between corresponding layer states and passes the retrieved examples with their reference answers to the same VLM. Across two VLMs and eight classification and visual question answering datasets, Raw achieves the highest average accuracy among training-free retrievers, exceeding the strongest baseline by 3.30 points on Qwen3-VL and 1.41 on InternVL3.5, with gains carrying over to Gemma-3-12B. When the same NICO++ image is asked about its object or its context, Raw retrieves largely different demonstrations for the two questions and outperforms an image retriever given task-matched demonstrations. A lightweight shared projection, TaskVICL-Proj, adds further gains on classification. Controls show that task text at extraction drives most of the gains, and attention analysis describes how it shifts attention toward the classified object. These results support the target VLM's internal states as a practical basis for task-aware demonstration retrieval.

replace VG3R: Video Generation Model for Feed-Forward 3D Reconstruction

Authors: Jingong Chen, Qingwen Zhang, Linlian Jiang, Xinxin Zuo, Yang Wang, Chulwoo Pack, Kyle Gao, Patric Jensfelt, Kwanghee Won

Abstract: Video generation models are widely used as 3D priors for novel view synthesis and 3D scene generation. Fine-tuned for perception, generation models also excel at dense prediction such as depth estimation. However, methods that apply video generation models to 3D reconstruction either predict depth without cameras or generate geometry and estimate cameras through many iterative denoising steps, which makes them slower. In this paper, we present $\textbf{VG3R}$, which turns a pretrained video generation model into a feed-forward 3D reconstruction model. Our key observation is that such a model already captures the depth and camera motion of a video, but not in the form required for reconstruction. We introduce two components: $\textit{clip-level shift prediction}$, which corrects depth distortion caused by the unknown shift in decoded inverse depth, and a $\textit{multi-layer camera head}$, which predicts per-frame cameras parameters by combining features across transformer blocks. Using Wan2.1 as its video backbone, VG3R reconstructs a scene and estimate the camera of every frame without iterative denoising. It achieves the lowest ATE and reconstruction errors among methods built on video generation models, runs about 4 and 14 times faster than Aether and Geo4D. VG3R also demonstrates competitive depth estimation and 3D reconstruction performance compared with geometry foundation models. Project page: https://github.com/KTH-RPL/VG3R

URLs: https://github.com/KTH-RPL/VG3R

replace Domain-Specific Self-Supervised Learning for Cuneiform Sign Representation

Authors: Asim Niaz, Morris Alper, Shai Gordin, Enrique Jim\'enez

Abstract: Recognizing cuneiform signs is challenging: sign shapes vary across historical periods, scribal practices, and preservation conditions, while expert annotations are scarce. We study whether domain-specific self-supervised pretraining yields better cuneiform sign representations than generic natural-image pretraining. We pretrain a masked autoencoder, CuneiMAE, on over 900K unlabeled sign crops and compare it with ImageNet-pretrained MAE, CLIP, and DINOv2 on a 218-class sign recognition benchmark. CuneiMAE learns substantially stronger frozen representations (43.7% linear-probe accuracy vs. 25.2% for ImageNet-MAE), with gains over ImageNet-MAE on cross-period transfer. Under fine-tuning, CuneiMAE outperforms ImageNet-pretrained baselines on our internal benchmark, with the largest gains when labels are scarce (+20.4 pp over ImageNet-MAE with 1% of labels). On an external benchmark, CuneiMAE reaches 69.9% top-1 accuracy after fine-tuning, about 8 pp above the ResNet-18 baseline from prior work. Explicit structural priors, such as edge-guided masking and part bottlenecks, do not improve over standard masked reconstruction. Overall, domain-specific self-supervision shows strong performance on cuneiform recognition tasks, particularly when labels are limited.

replace PSC-Gen: Physical Sequence Conditioning for Video Generation via Agentic Visual Planning

Authors: Wonsik Shin, Jinju Jang, Junhoo Lee, Yeonjin Chang, Jimyeong Kim, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Recent advances in video diffusion models have enabled the synthesis of high-fidelity, visually compelling videos. However, despite being trained on internet-scale video data, understanding underlying physical laws and generating physically plausible dynamics remain challenging. To mitigate this, conditioning video generators on explicit physical reasoning has emerged as a promising direction. Nevertheless, existing conditioning approaches often fail to incorporate detailed physical guidance into generation, resulting in physically implausible motion or visual artifacts. We propose PSC-Gen, a training-free framework that employs an agentic approach to synthesize visual conditions and inject their core dynamics via low-frequency filtering. By iteratively planning physical states and verifying generated keyframes, our Physics Agent constructs visually and physically consistent keyframe sequences. We then interpolate these keyframes into a continuous physical sequence and inject only its low-frequency components, steering the video model to preserve physical dynamics while synthesizing fine visual details. Qualitative and quantitative evaluations across physics-informed and robotic manipulation benchmarks demonstrate that PSC-Gen faithfully follows instructions and generates physically plausible videos.

replace Seeing vs. Knowing: Counterfactual Supervision and Correction for 3D-Aware Object Editing

Authors: Jian Zahng, Zhijun Zhang

Abstract: Rotating, translating, or rescaling an object in a photograph requires revealing unseen surfaces, preserving visible appearance, and updating shadows and reflections. Reconstructed 3D assets, rendered at the target pose as a 3D state, provide explicit geometric guidance, but their imperfect textures may conflict with the photograph, while standard paired training does not explicitly teach the editor which source to trust. To address this, we propose counterfactual state supervision, which constructs controlled photo--state conflicts through hidden-surface repainting, hidden geometry with re-rendered shadows and reflections, and visible-surface texture replacement. This teaches the editor to follow the 3D state for unseen content while preserving photograph-visible appearance. However, learning this distinction does not ensure that it is maintained during sampling. We therefore introduce symmetric counterfactual correction, which uses state-side and photo-side twins, which share the photograph and the state respectively, to construct directed latent perturbations toward condition-inconsistent targets and train recovery toward the target specified by the current condition. Both strategies operate only during training, requiring no architectural changes or additional inference steps. Experiments on OBJect and SpatialEdit-Bench demonstrate strong spatial editing performance and content preservation, while controlled ablations show improved source selection and sustained responsiveness to conditioning during sampling.

replace PROPS: Can Visual Generators Keep the Current Look of a Prop?

Authors: Xiangzhao Hao, Dianyi Wang, Keyan Zhou, Yiqian Zhang, Zefeng Zhang, Bailin Li, Kun Zhan, Junchi Yan

Abstract: Many generation tasks draw the next image from a visual history, such as storyboards, illustrated stories and multi-turn editing. A new request often states what happens next without restating how earlier objects look. Instead of recovering an object's current look from the history, generators often redraw it from the request alone or bring back an outdated look, and the error can carry into later steps. We call this failure prop drift. Existing evaluations rarely isolate it, because similarity to the history rewards copying and a judge that sees a single history cannot tell a look recovered from that history from one the generator happens to prefer. We introduce PROPS, a benchmark of 2,257 pairs of twin histories that end on the same request but show one prop in two different looks. A pair passes only if the generator draws the matching look on each side and carries out the request, so a look preferred by the generator or the judge passes at most one side. All 34 open and closed systems we test show prop drift, and even systems that carry out the requested action on most sides often lose the current look of the prop. Swapping the planners and editors of agents further shows that both history understanding and image generation limit agent performance. We also build PROPS-Train, 80,250 training chains in four tracks whose targets recover an earlier state or carry scripted changes forward, and fine-tuning on it clearly improves all four tested models. Both datasets will be publicly released.

replace One Size Doesn't Fit All: Input Adaptive Class Activation Mapping for Improved Visual Explanations

Authors: Ayesha Sahar, Tahir Mustafa Madni, Tehseen Zia, David Windridge, Uzair Iqbal Janjua, Waleed Umar

Abstract: Despite their widespread use in tasks such as image classification, object detection and semantic segmentation, the limited interpretability of Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) can undermine trust and hinder adoption in safety-critical applications. Explainable artificial intelligence (XAI) methods seek to address this by providing human-understandable explanations for model predictions. Class Activation Maps (CAM) constitute one such widely used XAI technique for visualizing discriminative regions. However, existing CAM methods may exhibit limitations in channel weighting, localization, and adaptability across network architectures potentially resulting in noisy or imprecise explanations. To address these issues, we propose ADAPT-CAM, a gradient-based, parameter-adaptive CAM framework. The method combines first-order gradient energy with activation-weighted second-order curvature to derive channel contributions. It further incorporates adaptive local contrast normalization and knee-guided soft channel selection to refine the resulting explanations. Importantly, key parameters are estimated independently for each input, allowing the framework to adapt to variations in feature representations across network architectures. We validate ADAPT-CAM on ImageNet and Oxford 102 Flowers across CNN and ViT architectures. Quantitative results demonstrate competitive or superior performance against established CAM methods across multiple evaluation metrics. In parallel, qualitative comparisons illustrate its ability to highlight discriminative image regions. Further parameter analysis and ablation experiments examine the behaviour and contributions of the individual adaptive components. These findings demonstrate the applicability of ADAPT-CAM across different architectures and datasets.

replace PixelOne: From Options to Pixels in a Single Forward for GUI Grounding

Authors: Kanghui Tian, Shuai Dong, Yuhan Zhu, Congcong Wang, Ji Zhao, Tianxiang Jiang, Zhixiong Zhang, Shengyuan Ding, Yi Wang, Jiaqi Wang, Yu Qiao

Abstract: GUI grounding requires precise clicks at low latency. Recent models such as GUI-Decisions and GUI-Swift predict coordinates in one forward pass using fixed global bins with local refinement. However, screenshot downsampling removes detail needed to locate small controls, while fixed bins cover larger pixel ranges at higher resolutions. We introduce PixelOne, which transfers spatial knowledge from a search teacher to a student that predicts clicks directly from screenshots at native resolution. The teacher uses VideoJEV's text-option scorer to select rows and columns and progressively zoom into candidate regions. The student inherits the teacher's parameters and matches its distributions over explored regions through spatial distillation alongside box supervision. At inference, the same decision head selects a visual token, and a lightweight pixel head predicts the click within it, all in one forward pass. A click-aligned loss encourages pixels inside the target box to score above those outside. Across four benchmarks, PixelOne-Pro-35B-A3B achieves 83.8% mean accuracy, the highest among the evaluated single-forward grounders, with a mean latency of 0.43 s per target.

replace SVUBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Proactive Streaming Video Understanding

Authors: Yuxuan Liang, Bohan Zhang, Haiyang Yu, Bocheng Hu, Ke Niu, Xu Li, Zhengtao Yao, Yan Wen, Haotian Chen, Xiaolei Chen, Jiacong Wang, Zhe Liu, Jingqun Tang, Benlei Cui, Fan Shi, Xiangyang Xue

Abstract: Proactive streaming video understanding requires models to interpret unfolding events, respond timely, and retrieve relevant historical evidence. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce SVUBench, a comprehensive proactive streaming video benchmark comprising 12,131 questions and 62,881 answer points across ten proactive tasks spanning Perception, Understanding, and Reasoning. Constructed through model-assisted annotation and human verification, SVUBench introduces two complementary interaction modes. Basic evaluates sustained proactive responses from video onset, while Trajectory requires historical recovery and continuous response updates following task activation during streaming. A Duration-Scaled Challenge further evaluates these capabilities across four video duration tiers. We develop the Proactive Response Score (PRS) with task-adaptive answer windows to jointly assess answer correctness, response timeliness, historical consistency, and content quality. Extensive experiments and analysis reveal the strengths and limitations of existing models, highlighting directions for advancing proactive streaming video understanding.

replace ViDiHand2: Unifying Video Diffusion Priors and Hand Geometry for 3D Hand Motion Reconstruction in the Wild

Authors: Yuxi Wang, Yufei Liu, Chengkai Jin, Qingyue Chen, Wenqi Ouyang, Tianyi Wei, Kerui Ren, Siyuan Huang, Zhiqi Shen, Hongsheng Li, Xingang Pan

Abstract: Learning robot manipulation from egocentric human videos requires accurate metric 3D hand motion. Recent methods leverage pretrained video diffusion model (VDM) priors to recover occluded hands directly from full-frame videos, but their appearance-based depth regression generalizes poorly to unseen cameras. In this work, we propose ViDiHand2, a geometry-driven framework that reformulates VDM-based hand depth estimation from direct regression into fine-grained geometric constraint solving. Specifically, we introduce Fine-Grained Geometric Lifting and Clip-Level Ray Fitting, which recover metric depth by enforcing geometric consistency between pixel-aligned 2D joints and the metric structure of MANO hands, while leveraging temporal observations across the clip. Since the lifting relies on these 2D joints, to improve robustness under occlusion and image truncation, we further develop Confidence-Gated Fusion to adaptively combine geometrically lifted joints with MANO predictions according to localization confidence. Finally, we enhance the VDM backbone to better capture spatial-temporal representations and produce the per-frame 2D joint and MANO predictions that both steps rely on. Extensive experiments on seven public datasets demonstrate that ViDiHand2 consistently outperforms 18 baselines, reducing in-the-wild absolute joint error by 42% and detection failures by 8.82x compared with the best baseline on each metric, while running at 57 FPS. The project page is available at https://vidihand2.github.io/.

URLs: https://vidihand2.github.io/.

replace Optimising VLM Harnesses to Localise Errors in Generated Videos

Authors: Ankit Yadav, Nikhil Cherian Kurian, Arpit Garg, Lingqiao Liu

Abstract: Identifying subtle errors in generated videos is an important step toward automated video generation, where errors must be detected to assess and refine generated clips. Recent work shows that pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) can localise such errors, but their effectiveness depends strongly on the workflow used: choices about which frames to inspect, which tools and model calls to make, and how to combine predictions can lead to substantially different results. We study VLM harnesses for video error localisation. Instead of relying on manually designed harnesses, we propose Harness Search and Merge (HSM), an algorithm that automatically constructs them by searching over executable evaluation programs. An LLM orchestrator refines candidate harnesses independently on random training subsets, records findings in a memory, and constructively merges complementary candidates. We evaluate HSM on Spotlight, a recently proposed benchmark for localising errors in generated videos. HSM achieves 0.234 $\pm$ 0.012 F1, exceeding the strongest benchmark strategy (0.173 $\pm$ 0.016), and retains its lead on an unseen video generator (0.213 $\pm$ 0.008 versus 0.140 $\pm$ 0.003). On IPV-Bench, it achieves strong localisation performance with lower false-alarm rates than existing optimisation baselines. Together, these results show that searching over complete VLM harnesses can outperform existing evaluation strategies, transfer to an unseen video generator, and reduce false alarms on real videos.

replace Token Pruning Needs a Protected Sink Token: Coherent Spatial Aggregation in Vision Transformers

Authors: Julian Wyatt, James Willoughby, Allison Clement, Irina Voiculescu

Abstract: Vision Transformers (ViTs) are a predominant architecture for computer vision, with token reduction emerging as a popular paradigm to improve their efficiency. Prior work compares reduction algorithms, but not how models respond to them, nor how their response changes with model depth. We propose Monte Carlo Token Pruning (MCTP), which estimates each token's impact on the prediction when tokens are explicitly removed during the forward pass. Applying MCTP to recent pretrained models, we observe that most, such as DINOv3, maintain a similar ranking of token importance at every depth (high Coherent Aggregation), while SigLIP 2 does not. This coherence requires predictions to survive pruning, which depends on keeping high-norm "sink" tokens: prefix tokens are kept by design, while prefix-free SigLIP 2 forms them on patch tokens, which pruning may remove. We show that in removing DINOv3's prefix tokens, its predictions and coherence collapse, while protecting only 5 tokens for SigLIP 2 cuts the token impact outside the object's bounding box by up to 62%. Beyond this, MCTP offers a post-hoc method to evaluate how a pretrained ViT responds to pruning, and suggests that token reduction methods should keep "sinks".

replace Modern Self-Supervised Vision Transformers Are Pareidolic

Authors: Zihang Lin, Huaiyuan Qin, Liangli Zhen, Qing Guo, Jun Liu, Yew-Soon Ong, Hongyuan Zhu

Abstract: In human perception, pareidolia is the tendency to perceive a meaningful pattern, such as a face, in a random or ambiguous stimulus. We find a similar phenomenon in modern self-supervised Vision Transformers (ViTs): on white noise, which contains neither objects nor spatial structure, a few directions of their features still form spatially coherent patterns across the patches, imposed by the features themselves. We call this behavior pareidolia, and the span of these directions the pareidolic subspace. On natural images, it holds most of the feature variance in DINOv2 and DINOv3 but little in DINO. This excess explains why DINOv2 and DINOv3, despite stronger dense features, fall far behind DINO in object emergence, the hallmark ability of self-supervised ViTs to identify the patches of an object on their own. Our experiments further show that scaling down their pareidolic subspaces steadily restores their ability to discover objects, so their object emergence is hindered rather than lost. These findings naturally lead to a training-free calibration: Pareidolic Subspace Calibration (PSC) measures the pareidolic subspace only on a noise input and then calibrates the features of natural inputs by scaling its directions down to the variance level of the remaining directions. Across diverse object emergence tasks, PSC improves DINOv2 and DINOv3 by up to 31.7 CorLoc on object discovery, 30.2 maxF on saliency detection and 59.9~$\mathcal{J}$ on video object segmentation. To identify the cause of pareidolia, we trace it block by block and find that it forms in the middle blocks of every self-supervised model we examine but stays strong to the last block only in models trained with a patch-level loss; controlled training confirms that an image-level loss alone lets it fade.

replace Lyris: Layout-Guided Long Video Generation with Persistent 3D Memory

Authors: Chen Wang, Jiahui Huang, Riccardo de Lutio, Alex Trevithick, Bryan Chu, Jiawei Ren, Laura Leal-Taix\'e, Shengyu Huang, Lingjie Liu, Haithem Turki

Abstract: Exploring a scene from as little as a single photo requires generating a long, camera-controlled walkthrough that extends far beyond what was captured, yet remains consistent upon return. Memory-based video generation methods preserve what they have already seen, but have no anchor beyond it, where the camera drifts from its path and a text prompt can name objects but not place them. Layout-conditioned methods provide such an anchor and precise control over what appears where, but rely on layouts that are typically authored by hand. Both limitations arise because, beyond what was captured, nothing anchors the video model unless a human authors one. To overcome both, we propose Lyris, a long video generation framework that never leaves the video model without an anchor: a persistent point-cloud memory where the scene has been observed or generated, and a 3D layout that a vision-language model authors where it has not. Our key insight is that the interface between the two models can be surprisingly coarse. A layout of one labeled 3D box per object is simple enough for a vision-language model to author (and for a user to edit), yet expressive enough for a video model to follow once rendered along the camera path. We demonstrate walkthroughs of over three minutes and 50 meters from a single photo, passing through doorways into rooms it never shows. When evaluated on minute-long round trips across four datasets, Lyris outperforms state-of-the-art methods by a wide margin, reducing camera error by 3.8x and improving consistency upon return by 22% on average.

replace-cross A Steerable Deep Network for Model-Free Diffusion MRI Registration

Authors: Gianfranco Cort\'es, Xiaoda Qu, Baba C. Vemuri

Abstract: Nonrigid registration is vital to medical image analysis but remains challenging for diffusion MRI (dMRI) due to its high-dimensional, spatio-angular dependence. We present a novel, geometric deep learning framework for model-free, nonrigid registration of raw dMRI data. We formulate registration in the native spatio-angular acquisition space under the joint action of 3D roto-translations, $\mathrm{SE}(3)$. Central to our framework is the equivalence between equivariant spatio-angular convolutions and 3D steerable convolutions on spherical-harmonic coefficient fields, which we exploit to construct an $\mathrm{SE}(3)$-equivariant U-Net. A by-product of this design choice is freedom from having to augment the data with roto-translated versions of itself. We also formulate a maximum mean discrepancy (MMD) loss between water-displacement distributions, evaluated in Fourier space as a weighted sum of squared signal differences, and extend it with a local contrast correction that reduces to a local correlation. In controlled comparisons, including a known deformation of real tissue, the raw signal yields markedly more accurate alignment than fractional anisotropy under the same optimizer. On held-out HCP subjects and on a held-out clinical-grade cohort (AOMIC) from a different scanner, our method improves anatomical overlap over ANTs SyN on fractional anisotropy, mrregister on fODFs, DDMReg and SynthMorph, without estimating any derived representation, and the steerable network matches a conventional U-Net with fewer parameters while being unaffected by grid rotations of its inputs. This work establishes a foundation for data-driven, geometry-aware dMRI registration directly in the acquisition space.

replace-cross Collision Risk Estimation via Loss Prediction in End-to-End Autonomous Driving

Authors: Ziliang Xiong, Shipeng Liu, Nathaniel Helgesen, Hongwei Li, Joakim Johnander, Per-Erik Forssen

Abstract: Collision risk estimation and avoidance play central roles in the safety of autonomous driving (AD) systems. Recently emerged end-to-end AD systems gain collision avoidance ability by minimizing losses to penalize planning trajectories that are too close to other objects. Despite a significant collision rate during testing, most end-to-end planners do not explicitly quantify the collision risk in their outputs. To address this, we introduce RiskMonitor, an efficient plug-and-play module that interprets planning and motion tokens from state-of-the-art end-to-end planners to estimate collision risk. Inspired by loss prediction based uncertainty quantification, RiskMonitor predicts whether the collision loss -- commonly adopted to train end-to-end planners -- is positive along planned waypoints, framing collision risk estimation as a binary classification task. Our token-driven method outperforms prediction-driven approaches, including deterministic rules, Gaussian mixture models, and Monte Carlo Dropout. When integrated with a simple braking policy, RiskMonitor improves collision avoidance ability by $66.5\%$ in a closed-loop test on safety-critical scenarios. These results demonstrate that monitoring collision risk using plan and motion tokens enhances the safety of end-to-end AD without retraining it.

replace-cross ViTrail: Transmission-Inference Co-Design for Vision Transformer-Based Mobile Video Analytics

Authors: Miao Zhang, Guanzhen Wu, Hao Fang, Yifei Zhu, Fangxin Wang, Ruixiao Zhang, Linfeng Shen, Jiangchuan Liu

Abstract: Edge-assisted mobile video analytics (MVA) relies on a tight device-edge feedback loop in which captured frames are transmitted for remote inference and the returned results are rendered on the mobile device. Many existing systems optimize frame transmission while treating server-side inference as a fixed cost. This separation becomes inefficient for vision transformer (ViT)-based dense prediction, where high-resolution inputs simultaneously increase network traffic and server computation. We present ViTrail, which co-designs transmission and ViT inference through a shared mixed-resolution representation. The device preserves task-relevant regions at full resolution and downsamples less informative regions before encoding. The server tokenizes each region at its transmitted resolution, so the same regional resolution assignment reduces both the upload payload and the number of tokens processed by the ViT. For dense prediction, ViTrail aligns regions with ViT patches and attention windows and restores a regular feature grid at a configurable stage. At runtime, it selects regional downsampling, compression quality, and the restoration point to maximize predicted installation accuracy, the accuracy of the rendered result when a new remote result is first used for rendering. We build a fully functional prototype on commodity mobile and edge devices and evaluate it in real time across 300 video-trace pairs with real-world 4G/5G traces. ViTrail achieves the highest mean rendering accuracy among the evaluated systems and leads each baseline on 81% to 95% of pairs, while reducing median end-to-end offloading latency by 38% against the most accurate baseline.

replace-cross Generative Modeling via Drifting

Authors: Mingyang Deng, He Li, Tianhong Li, Yilun Du, Kaiming He

Abstract: Generative modeling can be formulated as learning a mapping f such that its pushforward distribution matches the data distribution. The pushforward behavior can be carried out iteratively at inference time, e.g., in diffusion/flow-based models. In this paper, we propose Drifting Models, a new generative modeling paradigm that evolves the pushforward distribution during training and naturally admits one-step inference. We introduce a drifting field that governs the sample movement and achieves equilibrium when the distributions match. This leads to a training objective that allows the neural network optimizer to evolve the distribution. In experiments, our one-step generator achieves state-of-the-art one-step results on ImageNet 256x256, with FID 1.54 in latent space and 1.61 in pixel space. We hope that our work opens up new opportunities for high-quality one-step generation.

replace-cross Online Monitoring Framework for Automotive Time Series Data using JEPA Embeddings

Authors: Alexander Fertig, Karthikeyan Chandra Sekaran, Lakshman Balasubramanian, Michael Botsch

Abstract: As autonomous vehicles are rolled out, measures must be taken to ensure their safe operation. In order to supervise a system that is already in operation, monitoring frameworks are frequently employed. These run continuously online in the background, supervising the system status and recording anomalies. This work proposes an online monitoring framework to detect anomalies in object state representations. Thereby, a key challenge is creating a framework for anomaly detection without anomaly labels, which are usually unavailable for unknown anomalies. replace-cross ProFed: Projection-Guided Personalized Federated Learning for Low-Dose CT Denoising

Authors: Anas Zafar, Muhammad Waqas, Amgad Muneer, Rukhmini Bandyopadhyay, Eman Showkatian, Jia Wu

Abstract: Low-dose computed tomography (CT) reduces radiation exposure but introduces noise and artifacts whose appearance depends on acquisition conditions. We present ProFed, a personalized federated framework that combines image-domain feature modulation with projection-domain supervision. The results support projection-guided supervision as a promising component of federated image restoration within this controlled setting; independent deployment validation and larger multi-institutional studies remain necessary.

replace-cross Seeking the Unfamiliar but Memorable: Conceptual Creativity as Meta-Learning

Authors: Mengye Ren

Abstract: What does it mean to create a new concept, rather than retrieve a familiar one? Repeatedly sampling a generative model at the same prompt produces variations with similar styles and typical content. We propose that creativity is the production of stimuli that are unfamiliar to an adaptive observer at first sight, but quickly learnable from a few exposures. We formalize this as a Creator-Appraiser pair: a Creator generates a candidate, an Appraiser adapts to it for a few inner-loop learning steps, and the Appraiser's improvement becomes the reward the Creator optimizes through. We instantiate the framework with diffusion as the Creator, an autoencoder Appraiser on MNIST, and a CLIP Appraiser with a low-rank adapter for natural images. With a frozen diffusion model and no additional language conditioning, the meta-learning gradient is enough to produce both stylistic variations and concept compositions beyond what the base model can generate on its own.

replace-cross Same Concept, Different Directions: Cross-Modal Feature Heterogeneity in Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Chungpa Lee, Jihoon Kwon, Kyle Min, Jy-yong Sohn

Abstract: Vision-language models map images and text into a joint embedding space. However, these embeddings often entangle multiple semantic features, which limits their interpretability and controllability. While sparse autoencoders have emerged as a useful tool for decomposing these embeddings into monosemantic features, their application to joint embedding spaces has largely relied on an implicit, untested assumption that semantically corresponding features share the same directions across modalities. In this paper, we challenge this assumption by characterizing discrepancies in feature directions for the same concept across image and text modalities, a phenomenon we term cross-modal feature heterogeneity. We demonstrate that this heterogeneity is a key driver of the modality split, where a shared concept activates different latents depending on the modality. This finding further reveals why aligning latent activations alone is insufficient to resolve the underlying feature mismatch. Motivated by this observation, we propose an approach that trains modality-specific sparse autoencoders to preserve each modality's feature geometry, and then aligns corresponding features post hoc. Our method improves reconstruction fidelity and enhances performance in cross-modal retrieval and concept-conditioned retrieval.

replace-cross Does Latent Planning Survive Point Clouds? Action-Conditioned JEPA World Models for Geometric Observations and Goals

Authors: Fabio F. Oberweger, Michael Schwingshackl, Markus Murschitz

Abstract: Latent action world models let agents plan new behaviors at test time by predicting how actions change the environment, and joint-embedding predictive architectures (JEPAs) do so by forecasting future latent states rather than pixels. Yet nearly all such models see the world through a camera, even though robotic manipulation is fundamentally geometric: in robotics goals for manipulation are traditionally specified by target object poses, not by images of the object once placed. We ask whether latent planning survives a shift from appearance to geometry, on the observation side as well as on the goal specifications side. To answer this, we extend the stable-worldmodel evaluation platform with simulated LiDAR-style raycast point clouds as a new sensor modality, and adapt three JEPA designs to point clouds: a frozen-encoder model built on Utonia features, a distribution-prior model based on LeWM, and an action-sensitive model based on Delta-JEPA. We further introduce a goal-encoding mechanism that constructs the goal latent from the current latent and a 3D target pose, removing the need for goal images or goal point clouds. A comparative evaluation of the different anti-collapse mechanisms shows that point-cloud world models can match their image-based counterparts, demonstrating that the modality shift from appearance to geometry is achievable. All models are released as open weights with open-source training and inference code, to make world-model planning accessible for LiDAR-driven and pose-directed robotic tasks.

replace-cross CRAD: Class-wise Reliability-Aware Distillation for Decentralized Heterogeneous Federated Learning

Authors: Baraa Bilbeisi, Mengchen Fan, Baocheng Geng, Qing Tian

Abstract: Conventional federated learning (FL) relies on parameter averaging, which forces clients to be doubly homogeneous: it demands an identical architecture and degrades under non-IID data. Real-world deployments usually break both assumptions. We sidestep both by building a decentralized knowledge distillation framework in which each client evaluates its peers' model snapshots on its own local data and distills from the resulting soft predictions. Because knowledge is transferred through the shared class posterior, clients are free to run different architectures; and because every teacher is evaluated on the student's own device, raw data never leaves the client, with no central server or public dataset required. Within this setting, we identify and address an under-examined problem: how to combine the peer teacher predictions. Existing methods, like uniform averaging, ignore how knowledge reliability varies across teachers and classes. We propose Class-wise Reliability-Aware Distillation (CRAD), which, per class, first discards teachers that disagree with the peer consensus and then takes a weighted average of the rest, weighting each teacher by its per-class reliability (precision, or inverse variance). Since the variance of an accuracy from $n$ samples scales as $1/n$, support enters automatically: among the teachers that survive filtering, a teacher is trusted for a class to the degree that it is both accurate and well-evidenced for it. On CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and PathMNIST, across heterogeneous architectures under severe non-IID skew, CRAD consistently outperforms competing methods in global accuracy. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/bbilbeis/CRAD.

URLs: https://github.com/bbilbeis/CRAD.

replace-cross Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory from User Traffic for Graphic Design Agent

Authors: Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav

Abstract: Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modular skill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools. Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.

replace-cross YUBI-STAG: Contact and Semantic-Rich Alignment for VLAs via Automated Video-Language Grounding

Authors: Masatoshi Tateno, Takehiko Ohkawa, Yueh-Hua Wu, Hanlong Li, Tatsuya Matsushima, Yoichi Sato, Kei Ota

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models acquire broad manipulation capabilities via large-scale pretraining, yet eliciting them through language requires fine-grained alignment between instructions and physical interactions. Existing robot demonstrations typically provide only coarse task descriptions, omitting how actions are executed, including which gripper acts, which object is contacted, and how it is grasped and moved. We introduce YUBI-STAG, a framework for Spatio-Temporal Annotation and Grounding that automatically enriches manipulation demonstrations with interaction-rich semantics to align pretrained VLAs with fine-grained manipulation language. Combining contact-object segmentation with vision-language models, YUBI-STAG annotates object identities, attributes and states, per-gripper actions, bimanual coordination, and spatially grounded interactions. To address YUBI-STAG's reliance on localized sequences and multi-stage VLM inference, we distill it into YUBI-VLM. YUBI-VLM directly recovers action structure and annotations from raw, unsegmented video in few inference calls and operates from wrist views alone. We evaluate both frameworks on YUBI-STAG-Bench across temporal, semantic, and spatial grounding tasks. YUBI-VLM retains much of YUBI-STAG's annotation accuracy with fewer inference calls and shorter runtime while generalizing to unseen manipulations. Finally, post-training VLA policies on these annotations aligns them with fine-grained language and contact-aware structure. Bimanual experiments demonstrate improved performance and instruction following, including control over object identity, acting gripper, target location, and spatial relations absent from original labels.

replace-cross On Agentic Manipulation Policies

Authors: Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Chen Chen, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/ for code and videos.

URLs: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/

replace-cross Off-Policy RL with Online Inference Tuning: 1st Place Solution to the CoRL 2026 RoboEval Challenge

Authors: Ilia Larchenko

Abstract: The CoRL 2026 RoboEval challenge has eight bimanual tasks, and it ranks a policy not only on whether it succeeds but on how: every stage of the task, done safely, efficiently and with both arms in coordination, scored against human demonstrations. My solution has two parts. The generic part, carried over from my LeHome challenge solution, is off-policy RL on a $\pi_{0.5}$-based policy that is also its own value function. It is trained with AWR and RECAP on advantages of the exact official score, in a loop where every rollout is at the same time a Thompson-sampling trial of about 20 inference parameters. Out of the box, it took the policy to 0.85 RDS on held-out seeds in two days (and it was still very far from convergence). The competition-specific part adds data that local exploration cannot find: generated demonstrations that reach the full reward, and recovery episodes that start from the policy's own failures. The submitted policy reaches 0.990 RDS on my held-out seeds and 0.987 on the official seeds, and it won first place in the challenge, with 0.988 in the organisers' final evaluation on 1,000 unpublished seeds per task.

replace-cross Visual Similarity in Adult Word Extension and Artificial Word Learning

Authors: Patricio M. Vera

Abstract: When two objects share a name, how much does their visual similarity explain, and what do the teaching examples add? We studied word extension across ten visual representations: eight trained encoders, a randomly initialized network, and raw pixels. Reanalyzing trial-level data from a large adult word-extension study (Lewis & Frank, 2018; 520 people), we computed each test image's distance to the teaching examples. Within the taught domain, greater distance was associated with less extension for all eight trained encoders and for the untrained network, so the association cannot be attributed only to what the encoders learned; raw pixels showed no negative association. In post hoc models that let the distance slope vary across images, seven of the eight trained encoders retained a negative estimate and five had 95% intervals entirely below zero; the interval of the reference encoder included zero. Whether trial order changes the distance slope, the frozen primary question, remained inconclusive. Example composition changed extension at comparable distances: after three examples, adults extended a word to 1.1% of superordinate items when the examples were subordinate and to 77.5% when they were superordinate. In artificial learners, words joining more distant categories were learned less accurately for six of the eight trained encoders. Every trained encoder correlated with human similarity measures from three datasets, and so did the untrained network, although far more weakly than the trained encoders other than MAE on two of them. In an exploratory comparison on the same images, no common threshold on any trained encoder reproduced the adults' narrow-to-broad gain (at most 66.67 vs. 76.43 percentage points). Cosine distance to the centroid of the teaching examples predicts part of word extension, but does not capture the effect of example composition.

replace-cross Asymmetric Optical Image Watermarking

Authors: Guang Yang

Abstract: Watermark detectors held by a provider cannot be checked by third parties, and a party that can detect a mark can usually also forge it. We study a two-layer image watermark in which the Fourier transform at the core of the pipeline is performed by a coherent optical 4F unit. Layer 1 carries an Ed25519 signature bound to the image content in Fourier magnitudes, so anyone with the public key verifies offline. Layer 2 is a key-defined carrier written by a secret phase hologram and read by a matched-filter correlator. The optics is a scalar-diffraction simulation with finite modulator bits, pixel aperture, pupil, defocus, misalignment, and sensor noise. No hardware is built. All criteria were fixed in a hashed specification before any optical result. The simulated optical signer reproduces the digital pipeline (field error 1.7e-15, bit agreement 99.997%), verifies at least 99.5% of 877 images under each of 8 post-sensor settings, one unattacked, and accepts none of 40,670 unmarked images. The optical carrier is key-selective: the matched key detects it on 99.8% of the unattacked images, and detection falls to 0% under a scale error of 1% and to 0% under a mask shift of 1 bin. Security rests on the signature and the content binding, for which we separate what is provable from what is measured.

replace-cross Hypnos: A Dual-Phase End-to-End Platform for Scalable Continual Learning at the Edge

Authors: Guilherme Guedes, Marco P. Apolinario, Charlotte Frenkel

Abstract: Continual learning (CL) enables neural networks to adapt to non-stationary data streams by mitigating catastrophic forgetting, offering a path to robust, private, and adaptable edge devices. Yet, porting CL to real-world edge devices remains challenging for two reasons: (i) online operation, where sensor data arrives one sample at a time and the device must adapt on-the-fly, and (ii) the low footprint of edge devices, which makes traditional error backpropagation training prohibitive. Inspired by the brain's wake and sleep cycles, the former for rapid adaptation with minimal resources and the latter for long-term consolidation, we introduce Hypnos, an open-source dual-phase platform for efficient CL at the edge. In the wake phase (e.g., daily operation), Hypnos relies on a low-footprint system-on-chip with a RISC-V microcontroller unit (MCU) targeting efficient inference and supporting lightweight gradient-free prototype updates. In the sleep phase (e.g., night-time charging), where wall-plug energy and larger memory budgets are available, a smart charger performs rehearsal-based gradient updates for consolidation. Deployed on a ZCU104 field-programmable gate array (FPGA) and synthesized in a 28-nm process, Hypnos is benchmarked on the CORe50 NICv2-391 protocol. It sets a new state-of-the-art accuracy of 78.6%-81.6% for edge CL systems, with an on-device latency of 7.79s-10.54s per sample depending on activation precision, and requires $3.8\times$ fewer learning steps and $22\times$ less learning compute than quantized latent replay at higher accuracy and comparable latency.

replace-cross Bradley-Terry Training Can Distort Outcome Probabilities of Initially Accurate Language World Models

Authors: Xinze Shi, Litian Zhang, Zhenjie Su, Zelong Yu, Yuting Zhang, Chaozhuo Li, Qihua Feng, Zifeng Kang, Zhengxuan Hu

Abstract: Comparative feedback, in which a judge says which of two candidate outcomes is closer to what happened, is a practical way to train language world models that assign probabilities to an action's outcomes. We show that, fitted with a Bradley-Terry objective, it need not teach the true outcome probabilities and can distort accurate ones. On a simulated robot push with enumerated outcome distributions, this objective takes a model that is accurate on unseen layouts (hazard error 2.4 percentage points, pp) and moves 33 to 43 pp of its probability onto outcomes that none of a condition's enumerated branches produces. The cause is the objective, not the comparisons: its optimum, computed before training, predicts where the model ends up, and a likelihood that knows the judge's rule learns from the same comparisons without the shift. The shift is robust: it recurs on unseen conditions, under five candidate laws and a three-fold denser enumeration, and about 29 pp remain without the physically impossible events. Its cost is conditional: with those events it inflates hazard probabilities, and a threshold inherited from the pre-feedback model costs about 3 pp of utility per decision on trained and 1 to 2 pp on unseen conditions, through false rejects; without them the utility loss averages below 1 pp over six seeds, though a small regret in action choice remains. Audits of readout, response and decision trace the effect from probabilities to actions.

replace-cross Visual Attribution Limits and Graded Provenance Auditing in Commercial Video-Generation APIs

Authors: Haocheng Mei, Yuchuan Tian, Yingte Shu, Chao Xu, Kai Han, Allen Chen, Yunhe Wang

Abstract: Commercial video-generation APIs bill by model and tier, while intermediaries could substitute cheaper models, lower tiers, or cached clips. What can a delivered file establish about its origin? We combine measurements of passive attribution and cryptographic provenance into a graded audit. We test attribution probes and zero-shot detectors under re-encoding, downscaling, and frame-rate changes, and survey C2PA and GB 45438 metadata across 19 commercial endpoints. Only authenticated provenance supports accusations: tier contradictions or duplicate signed instances under fresh-generation contracts, excluding retries and idempotent returns. Unverifiable signed-endpoint deliveries trigger original-file demands under pass-through clauses; archive-reuse and delivery-parameter screens support review only. Six-model container and bitstream accuracy falls from 83.9% on raw files to 45.3% after uniform re-encoding, using different feature sets. Subsampling real references from 24 to 3 fps raises pooled D3 AUROC from 0.845 to 0.988. Only BytePlus authenticates tier claims under our pinned policy, including 187/187 audited deliveries across three Seedance tiers; tested remuxing and re-encoding leave processed Seedance files unverifiable. The audit identifies authenticated contradictions in 100/100 raw 720p signed tier-swap cases, with zero observed false flags on the tested honest pools.

replace-cross HumanoidPose: Monocular 3D Pose Estimation for Humanoid Robots

Authors: Jinlin Gan, Mingzhe Li, Junsheng Zhang, Zhibo Zhou, Jiahan Geng, Yuhua Luo, Changwang Zhang, Chenglu Wen, Lan Xu, Siqi Shen, Cheng Wang

Abstract: As humanoid robots increasingly enter real-world environments, recovering their 3D whole-body pose from monocular imagery is becoming increasingly important for external state perception, motion analysis, and robot learning. However, Humanoid Robot Pose Estimation (HRPE) lies at the intersection of conventional Robot Pose Estimation (RPE) and Human Pose Estimation (HPE), while not being fully addressed by either paradigm: RPE primarily focuses on non-humanoid robots, particularly fixed-base manipulators, whereas HPE is built upon human-specific skeletal topologies, body models, and motion priors. Despite recent progress in humanoid 2D keypoint detection and HPE adaptation, humanoid-native 3D pose recovery remains insufficiently studied, and a dedicated benchmark with suitable training data and evaluation protocols is still lacking. To address this gap, we introduce Humanoids, a benchmark for monocular 3D humanoid robot pose estimation, and propose HumanoidPose, a framework for this task. Humanoids provides synthetic 3D supervision across diverse humanoid embodiments, manually annotated real-world 2D data, and real-world RGB sequences paired with robot states for 3D evaluation on a physical Roban humanoid. HumanoidPose combines human-inspired visual perception with robot articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. Experiments on Humanoids and the DHRP-H1 benchmark demonstrate strong performance in articulation recovery and 3D pose estimation, including zero-shot transfer to real-world 2D and 3D evaluation. Ablation studies further show the contribution of robot-native articulation modeling and explicit kinematic grounding. The dataset and code will be publicly released to support future research in the community.

replace-cross Policy-intrinsic Demonstration Curation

Authors: Sunjae Jeong, Geunhyeok Yu, Dowoon Lee, Hyoseok Hwang

Abstract: Fine-tuning a robot policy for a new target task requires target demonstrations, but only a few are available. Demonstrations of other tasks can fill this gap, and demonstration curation selects the useful ones among these candidates. Retrieval-based curation selects the candidates that are most similar to the target demonstrations. Since it does not use the policy, any candidate that looks similar receives a high score. Performance-aware curation therefore estimates the effect of each candidate on the policy. However, this requires training on the candidates and takes a long time. We propose a third branch, policy-intrinsic curation, which uses the policy and still takes little time. It estimates this effect with forward passes of the pretrained policy and needs no training. We instantiate this branch with PICKER (Policy-Intrinsic Curation from Known Errors). It selects the candidates on which the policy makes errors that agree with those on the target demonstrations. PICKER reaches success rates comparable to those of performance-aware curation and takes about as long as retrieval-based curation. Its selection is also stable across seeds.

replace-cross Reinforcing Diffusion Models by Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching

Authors: Yihong Luo

Abstract: Forward diffusion reinforcement learning (RL)---enabling deterministic ODE sampling without trajectory backpropagation---has grown increasingly popular for efficiently aligning diffusion models. However, we reveal that existing methods essentially rely on signed reward-weighted regression (RWR), regressing the trainable policy velocity onto rollouts from a behavior distribution. Unbiased baseline subtraction implicitly assumes that the policy velocity equals the behavior marginal velocity---an idealized assumption violated under classifier-free guidance (CFG) rollouts, offline/off-policy updates, and imperfect online self-consistency. This behavior--policy mismatch breaks baseline invariance and injects baseline-induced gradient drift, destabilizing training. To resolve this issue, we propose Instantaneous Trajectory Distribution Matching (iTDM), taking the infinitesimal limit of Trajectory Distribution Matching toward a time-local reward-guided target via the posterior-score identity to yield a sample-level gradient anchored at the behavior velocity without learning an additional reward-tilted target score. Tracking this velocity with an online auxiliary denoiser guarantees exact baseline invariance at the auxiliary optimum, effectively suppressing baseline drift across both online and offline RL. Extensive experiments across compositional generation, text rendering, and preference alignment show that iTDM achieves state-of-the-art in-domain and out-of-domain performance across group-wise online/offline RL and pairwise offline RL. Notably, on the challenging GenEval2 benchmark, iTDM converges nearly 3\times faster than competing methods while scaling smoothly to 0.87 without collapse. Furthermore, iTDM scales effectively to the 5.6B FLUX.3 model, achieving better performance across both in-domain and unseen metrics. Code is available at https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM

URLs: https://github.com/Luo-Yihong/iTDM

replace-cross WeaveVLA: 3D Relational Grounding for Vision-Language-Action Models

Authors: Zien Wang, Yuhao Pan, Yunfeng Fan, Nan Cheng, Xin Xia, Wenchao Xu

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models leverage pretrained vision-language representations for instruction-conditioned robot control, yet their understanding of task-relevant 3D relations and how these relations evolve during manipulation remains largely implicit. To address this challenge, we introduce WeaveVLA, a framework that enhances VLA action generation by explicitly modeling task-relevant relative 3D relations and maintaining relational context across observations. WeaveVLA employs a task-conditioned sparse graph neural network to encode relative 3D relations among objects, the gripper, and nearby references. Pair-specific recurrent edge states integrate geometric evidence across observations to maintain evolving spatial context. A learned-query readout summarizes the updated graph representations into compact relational tokens, which condition the action expert alongside the original vision-language context. An auxiliary geometry reconstruction objective further encourages spatial structure preservation. We instantiate WeaveVLA on StarVLA-GR00T and $\pi_{0.5}$, achieving over 98% average success on LIBERO with both backbones. On LIBERO-Plus, WeaveVLA improves success from 74.9% to 82.0% and from 85.7% to 87.8%, respectively, with additional improvements on CALVIN D$\rightarrow$D. On three real-world tasks, the StarVLA-GR00T-based variant improves average success from 55.6% to 70.0% under standard conditions and from 33.3% to 53.3% under OOD perturbations, surpassing the $\pi_{0.5}$ baseline in both settings.
